  • Running a command with different standard errorrange:
  - command: paexec -t ssh -c ~/bin/toupper -n 'server1 server2' < tasks
    parallel: parallel --pipe -n1 -S server1,server2 ~/bin/toupper < tasks
  - command: paexec -lr -t ssh -c ~/bin/toupper -n 'server1 server2' < tasks
    parallel: |
      parallel --tagstring {#} --pipe -n1 -S server1,server2 ~/bin/toupper < tasks
      cat tasks | parallel --tagstring {#} --pipe -n1 -S server1,server2 --plus \
        ~/bin/toupper '| perl -pe "s/^/{sshlogin}/"'  < tasks
  - command: paexec -n +4 -c ~/bin/toupper < tasks
    parallel: parallel -j4 --pipe -n1 ~/bin/toupper < tasks
  - command: paexec -x -t ssh -n 'server1 server2' -c "awk 'BEGIN {print toupper(ARGV[1])}' " < tasks
    parallel: parallel  -S 'server1,server2' "awk 'BEGIN {print toupper(ARGV[1])}'" < tasks
  - command: paexec -x -C -t ssh -n 'server1 server2' awk 'BEGIN {print toupper(ARGV[1])}' < tasks
    parallel: parallel -S 'server1,server2' -q awk 'BEGIN {print toupper(ARGV[1])}' < tasks
  - command: paexec -Z240 -x -t ssh -n 'server1 badhostname server2' -c "awk 'BEGIN {print toupper(ARGV[1])}' " < tasks
    parallel: parallel --filter-hosts -S 'server1,badhostname,server2' "awk 'BEGIN {print toupper(ARGV[1])}'" < tasks
  - command: |
      #!/usr/bin/env sh
      while read task; do
         banner -f M "$task" | pv -qL 300
         echo "$PAEXEC_EOT" # end-of-task marker
      done
      cat tasks
      paexec
      paexec -l -mt='SE@X-L0S0!&' -c ~/bin/pbanner -n +2 < tasks |
        paexec_reorder -mt='SE@X-L0S0!&'
    parallel: |
      paexec -y -lc ~/bin/pbanner -n+2 < tasks | paexec_reorder -y
      paexec -l -x -c banner -n+2 < tasks
      parallel --pipe -n1 -j2 ~/bin/pbanner < tasks
  - command: |
      #!/usr/bin/awk -f

      {
         print "build " $0
         print "success" # build succeeded! (paexec -ms=)
         print ""        # end-of-task marker
         fflush()        # we must flush stdout
      }
      paexec -g -l -c ~/bin/pkg_builder -n 'server2 server1' \
        -t ssh < ~/tmp/packages_to_build | paexec_reorder
    parallel: |
      # GNU Parallel cannot postpone jobs that depend on another.
      # In some cases this will work
      tsort ~/tmp/packages_to_build | parallel -S server2,server1 \
        --pipe -n1 ~/bin/pkg_builder
  - command: |
      #!/usr/bin/awk -f

      {
         print "build " $0
         if ($0 == "devel/gmake")
            print "failure" # Oh no...
            exit 255     # Exit value needed for GNU Parallel
         else
            print "success" # build succeeded!

         print ""        # end-of-task marker
         fflush()        # we must flush stdout
      }
      paexec -gl -c ~/bin/pkg_builder -n 'server2 server1' \
        -t ssh < ~/tmp/packages_to_build | paexec_reorder
    parallel: |
      # GNU Parallel cannot refrain from starting jobs, that depend on others
      # In some cases this will work
      tsort ~/tmp/packages_to_build | parallel -S server2,server1 \
        --halt now,fail=1 --pipe -n1 ~/bin/pkg_builder
  - command: |
      #!/usr/bin/awk -f

      {
         "hostname -s" | getline hostname
         print "build " $0 " on " hostname

         if (hostname == "server1" && $0 == "textproc/gsed")
            exit 139
            # Damn it, I'm dying...
            # Exit value is needed by GNU Parallel
         else
            print "success" # Yes! :-)

         print ""        # end-of-task marker
         fflush()        # we must flush stdout
      }

      paexec -gl -Z300 -t ssh -c ~/bin/pkg_builder \
        -n 'server2 server1' < ~/tmp/packages_to_build |
        paexec_reorder > result
    parallel: |
      # GNU Parallel retries a job on another server, if --retries > 1
      tsort ~/tmp/packages_to_build | parallel -S server2,server1 \
        --halt now,fail=1 --retries 2 --pipe -n1 ~/bin/pkg_builder
  - command: ls -1 *.wav | paexec -x -c 'flac -s' -n+3 >/dev/null
    parallel: ls -1 *.wav | parallel -j3 flac -s >/dev/null
  - command: ls -1 *.wav | paexec -ixC -n+3 oggenc -Q | grep .
    parallel: ls -1 *.wav | parallel -j3 'echo {}; oggenc -Q {}' | grep .
  - command: |
      #!/bin/sh
      # $1 -- task given on input
      if test $1 = huge; then
          sleep 6
      else
          sleep 1
      fi
      echo "task $1 done"
      printf 'small1\nsmall2\nsmall3\nsmall4\nsmall5\nhuge\n' |
        time -p paexec -c ~/bin/calc -n +2 -xg | grep -v success
    parallel: |
      printf 'small1\nsmall2\nsmall3\nsmall4\nsmall5\nhuge\n' |
        time -p parallel -j2 ~/bin/calc | grep -v success
  - command: |
      printf 'small1\nsmall2\nsmall3\nsmall4\nweight: huge 6\n' |
        time -p paexec -c ~/bin/calc -n +2 -x -W1 | grep -v success
    parallel: |
      # GNU Parallel does not support weighted jobs.
      # It can be simulated by sorting:
      printf 'small1\nsmall2\nsmall3\nsmall4\nweight: huge 6\n' |
        perl -pe 's/^weight: (.*) (\d+)/$2 $1/ or s/^/1 /' |
        sort -nr | time parallel ~/bin/calc '{=s/^\d* //=}' |
        grep -v success

paexec_examples:
  - title: all_substr
    command: paexec -lpe -c "`pwd`/cmd" -n +3 < My\ brother\'s\ 12\" records
      ls | map 'echo %; wc %'
    parallel_equivalent: ls | parallel 'echo {}; wc {}'
    map_partial_success: ls | map 'echo % does not work "%"'
    explanation: |
      This means that many UTF-8 characters will be rejected. This is by design. From the web page: "As such, programs that quietly handle them, with no warnings at all, are doing their users a disservice."
  delay:
    map_behavior: "map delays each job by 0.01 s. This can be emulated by using parallel --delay 0.01."
  progress_indicator:
    map_behavior: "map prints '+' on stderr when a job starts, and '-' when a job finishes. This cannot be disabled. parallel has --bar if you need to see progress."
  replacement_strings:
    config_suggestion: |
      parallel's replacement strings can be simulated in GNU parallel by putting this in ~/.parallel/config:
      --rpl '%'
      --rpl '%D $_=Q(::dirname($_));'
      --rpl '%B s:.*/::;s:\.[^/.]+$::;'
      --rpl '%E s:.*\.::'
  command_line_parsing:
    map_behavior: "map does not have an argument separator on the command line, but uses the first argument as command. This makes quoting harder which again may affect readability. Compare:"
    map_example: map -p 2 'perl -ne '\''/^\S+\s+\S+$/ and print $ARGV,"\\n"'\''' *
    parallel_example: parallel -q perl -ne '/^\S+\s+\S+$/ and print $ARGV,"\n"' ::: *
  context_replace:
    map_behavior: "map can do multiple arguments with context replace, but not without context replace:"
    parallel_example: parallel --xargs echo 'BEGIN{'{}'}END' ::: 1 2 3
    map_example: map "echo 'BEGIN{'%'}END'" 1 2 3
  grouping:
    map_behavior: "map has no support for grouping. So this gives the wrong results:"
    parallel_example: |
      parallel perl -e '\$a=\\"1{}\\"x100000000\;print\ \$a,\\"\\\\n\\\\n\\" ' '>' {} \
        ::: a b c d e f
      ls -l a b c d e f
      parallel -kP4 -n1 grep 1 ::: a b c d e f > out.par
    map_example: |
      map -n1 -p 4 'grep 1' a b c d e f > out.map-unbuf
      map -n1 -p 4 'grep --line-buffered 1' a b c d e f > out.map-linebuf
      map -n1 -p 1 'grep --line-buffered 1' a b c d e f > out.map-serial
      ls -l out*
      md5sum out*

map_website_examples:
  - command: ls *.gif | map convert % %B.png         # default max-args: 1
    parallel: ls *.gif | parallel convert {} {.}.png
  - command: map "mkdir %B; tar -C %B -xf %" *.tgz   # default max-args: 1
    parallel: parallel 'mkdir {.}; tar -C {.} -xf {}' :::  *.tgz
  - command: ls *.gif | map cp % /tmp                # default max-args: 100
    parallel: ls *.gif | parallel -X cp {} /tmp
  - command: ls *.tar | map -n 1 tar -xf %
    parallel: ls *.tar | parallel tar -xf
  - command: map "cp % /tmp" *.tgz
    parallel: parallel cp {} /tmp ::: *.tgz
  - command: map "du -sm /home/%/mail" alice bo
    parallel: parallel "du -sm /home/{}'/mail'" ::: alice bob
b carol
6$ parallel "du -sm /home/{}/mail" ::: alice bob carol
o si prefieres ejecutar un solo trabajo con múltiples argumentos:
6$ parallel -Xj1 "du -sm /home/{}/mail" ::: alice bob carol
7$ cat /etc/passwd | map -d: 'echo user %1 has shell %7'
7$ cat /etc/passwd | parallel --colsep : 'echo user {1} has shell {7}'
8$ export MAP_MAX_PROCS=$(( `nproc` / 2 ))
8$ export PARALLEL=-j50%

https://github.com/sitaramc/map (Última comprobación: 2020-05)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE ladon Y GNU Parallel

ladon puede ejecutar múltiples trabajos en paralelo sobre archivos.

ladon solo funciona en archivos y la única forma de especificar archivos es usando una cadena de globbing entrecomillada (como \*.jpg). No es posible listar los archivos manualmente.

Como cadenas de reemplazo utiliza FULLPATH DIRNAME BASENAME EXT RELDIR RELPATH

Estos pueden ser simulados usando GNU parallel poniendo esto en ~/.parallel/config:

--rpl 'FULLPATH $_=Q($_);chomp($_=qx{readlink -f $_});'
--rpl 'DIRNAME $_=Q(::dirname($_));chomp($_=qx{readlink -f $_});'
--rpl 'BASENAME s:.*/::;s:\.[^/.]+$::;'
--rpl 'EXT s:.*\.::'
--rpl 'RELDIR $_=Q($_);chomp(($_,$c)=qx{readlink -f $_;pwd});
       s:\Q$c/\E::;$_=::dirname($_);'
--rpl 'RELPATH $_=Q($_);chomp(($_,$c)=qx{readlink -f $_;pwd});
       s:\Q$c/\E::;'

ladon maneja mal los nombres de archivo que contienen " y saltos de línea, y falla para salidas mayores de 200k:

ladon '*' -- seq 36000 | wc

EJEMPLOS DEL MANUAL DE ladon

Se asume que los '--rpl' anteriores están puestos en ~/.parallel/config y que se ejecuta bajo un shell que soporta globbing '**' (como zsh):

1$ ladon "**/*.txt" -- echo RELPATH
1$ parallel echo RELPATH ::: **/*.txt
2$ ladon "~/Documents/**/*.pdf" -- shasum FULLPATH >hashes.txt
2$ parallel shasum FULLPATH ::: ~/Documents/**/*.pdf >hashes.txt
3$ ladon -m thumbs/RELDIR "**/*.jpg" -- convert FULLPATH \
     -thumbnail 100x100^ -gravity center -extent 100x100 \
     thumbs/RELPATH
3$ parallel mkdir -p thumbs/RELDIR\; convert FULLPATH
     -thumbnail 100x100^ -gravity center -extent 100x100 \
     thumbs/RELPATH ::: **/*.jpg
4$ ladon "~/Music/*.wav" -- lame -V 2 FULLPATH DIRNAME/BASENAME.mp3
4$ parallel lame -V 2 FULLPATH DIRNAME/BASENAME.mp3 ::: ~/Music/*.wav

https://github.com/danielgtaylor/ladon (Última comprobación: 2024-06)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE jobflow Y GNU Parallel

Resumen (ver leyenda arriba):

I1 - - - - - I7
- - M3 - - (M6)
O1 O2 O3 - O5 O6 (O7) - - O10
E1 - - - - E6 -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

jobflow puede ejecutar múltiples trabajos en paralelo.

Al igual que xargs, la salida de los trabajos de jobflow ejecutándose en paralelo se mezcla por defecto. jobflow puede hacer un buffer en archivos con -buffered (colocado en /run/shm), pero estos no se limpian si jobflow muere inesperadamente (por ejemplo, con Ctrl-C). Si la salida total es grande (en orden de RAM+swap) puede causar que el sistema se ralentice y eventualmente se quede sin memoria.

Al igual que xargs, la redirección y los comandos compuestos requieren envolverse con bash -c.

Las líneas de entrada pueden tener como máximo 4096 bytes.

jobflow es más rápido que GNU parallel pero unas 6 veces más lento que parallel-bash.

jobflow no tiene equivalente para --pipe, o --sshlogin.

jobflow permite establecer límites de recursos en los trabajos en ejecución. Esto puede ser emulado por GNU parallel usando ulimit de bash:

jobflow -limits=mem=100M,cpu=3,fsize=20M,nofiles=300 myjob
parallel 'ulimit -v 102400 -t 3 -f 204800 -n 300 myjob'

EJEMPLOS DEL README DE jobflow

1$ cat things.list | jobflow -threads=8 -exec ./mytask {}
1$ cat things.list | parallel -j8 ./mytask {}
2$ seq 100 | jobflow -threads=100 -exec echo {}
2$ seq 100 | parallel -j100 echo {}
3$ cat urls.txt | jobflow -threads=32 -exec wget {}
3$ cat urls.txt | parallel -j32 wget {}
4$ find . -name '*.bmp' | \
     jobflow -threads=8 -exec bmp2jpeg {.}.bmp {.}.jpg
4$ find . -name '*.bmp' | \
     parallel -j8 bmp2jpeg {.}.bmp {.}.jpg
5$ seq 100 | jobflow -skip 10 -count 10
5$ seq 100 | parallel --filter '{1} > 10 and {1} <= 20' echo
5$ seq 100 | parallel echo '{= $_>10 and $_<=20 or skip() =}'

https://github.com/rofl0r/jobflow (Última comprobación: 2024-06)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE gargs Y GNU Parallel

gargs puede ejecutar múltiples trabajos en paralelo.

Versiones antiguas guardan la salida en memoria. Esto causa que sea extremadamente lento cuando la salida es mayor que la RAM física, y puede causar que el sistema se quede sin memoria.

Ver más detalles sobre esto en man parallel_design.

Versiones más nuevas guardan la salida en archivos, pero dejan los archivos en $TMPDIR si se interrumpe.

La salida a stderr (error estándar) se cambia si el comando falla.

EJEMPLOS DEL SITIO WEB DE gargs

1$ seq 12 -1 1 | gargs -p 4 -n 3 "sleep {0}; echo {1} {2}"
1$ seq 12 -1 1 | parallel -P 4 -n 3 "sleep {1}; echo {2} {3}"
2$ cat t.txt | gargs --sep "\s+" \
     -p 2 "echo '{0}:{1}-{2}' full-line: \'{}\'"
2$ cat t.txt | parallel --colsep "\\s+" \
     -P 2 "echo '{1}:{2}-{3}' full-line: \'{}\'"

https://github.com/brentp/gargs (Última comprobación: 2016-08)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE orgalorg Y GNU Parallel

orgalorg puede ejecutar el mismo trabajo en múltiples máquinas. Esto está relacionado con --onall y --nonall.

orgalorg soporta ingresar la contraseña SSH, siempre que sea la misma para todos los servidores. GNU parallel aboga por usar ssh-agent en su lugar, pero es posible emular el comportamiento de orgalorg estableciendo SSHPASS y usando --ssh "sshpass ssh".

Para facilitar la emulación, haz un alias simple:

alias par_emul="parallel -j0 --ssh 'sshpass ssh' --nonall --tag --lb"

Si quieres proporcionar una contraseña ejecuta:

SSHPASS=`ssh-askpass`

o establece la contraseña directamente:

SSHPASS=P4$$w0rd!

Si lo anterior está configurado, entonces puedes hacer:

orgalorg -o frontend1 -o frontend2 -p -C uptime
par_emul -S frontend1 -S frontend2 uptime
orgalorg -o frontend1 -o frontend2 -p -C top -bid 1
par_emul -S frontend1 -S frontend2 top -bid 1
orgalorg -o frontend1 -o frontend2 -p -er /tmp -n \
  'md5sum /tmp/bigfile' -S bigfile
par_emul -S frontend1 -S frontend2 --basefile bigfile \
  --workdir /tmp md5sum /tmp/bigfile

orgalorg tiene un indicador de progreso para la transferencia de un archivo. GNU parallel no lo tiene.

https://github.com/reconquest/orgalorg (Última comprobación: 2016-08)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE Rust parallel(mmstick) Y GNU Parallel

Rust parallel se enfoca en la velocidad. Es casi tan rápido como xargs, pero no tan rápido como parallel-bash. Implementa algunas características de GNU parallel, pero carece de muchas funciones. Todas estas fallan:

# Leer argumentos de archivo
parallel -a file echo
# Cambiar el delimitador
parallel -d _ echo ::: a_b_c_

Estos hacen algo diferente a GNU parallel

# -q para proteger $ y espacios entre comillas
parallel -q perl -e '$a=shift; print "$a"x100000000' ::: a b c
# Generación de combinaciones de entradas
parallel echo {1} {2} ::: red green blue ::: S M L XL XXL
# {= expresión perl =} cadena de reemplazo
parallel echo '{= s/new/old/ =}' ::: my.new your.new
# --pipe
seq 100000 | parallel --pipe wc
# Argumentos enlazados
parallel echo ::: S M L :::+ sml med lrg ::: R G B :::+ red grn blu
# Ejecutar diferentes dialectos de shell
zsh -c 'parallel echo \={} ::: zsh && true'
csh -c 'parallel echo \$\{\} ::: shell && true'
bash -c 'parallel echo \$\({}\) ::: pwd && true'
# Rust parallel no empieza hasta que se lee el último argumento
(seq 10; sleep 5; echo 2) | time parallel -j2 'sleep 2; echo'
tail -f /var/log/syslog | parallel echo

La mayoría de los ejemplos del libro GNU Parallel 2018 no funcionan, por lo tanto, Rust parallel no está ni cerca de ser un reemplazo compatible.

Rust parallel no tiene facilidades remotas.

Usa /tmp/parallel para archivos temporales y no limpia si se termina abruptamente. Si otro usuario en el sistema usa Rust parallel, entonces /tmp/parallel tendrá permisos incorrectos y Rust parallel fallará. Un usuario malicioso puede configurar los permisos correctos y enlazar el archivo de salida a uno de los archivos del usuario y la próxima vez que el usuario use Rust parallel sobrescribirá este archivo.

attacker$ mkdir /tmp/parallel
attacker$ chmod a+rwX /tmp/parallel
# Enlazar al archivo que el atacante quiere poner a cero
attacker$ ln -s ~victim/.important-file /tmp/parallel/stderr_1
victim$ seq 1000 | parallel echo
# Este archivo ahora está sobrescrito con stderr de 'echo'
victim$ cat ~victim/.important-file

Si /tmp/parallel se llena durante la ejecución, Rust parallel no lo informa, sino que termina con éxito, ¡arriesgando así la pérdida de datos!

https://github.com/mmstick/parallel (Última comprobación: 2016-08)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE parallelion Y GNU Parallel

Resumen (ver leyenda arriba):

- (I2) - I4 - - -
M1 - M3 - - M6
- O2 O3 - O5 (O6) - x x
E1 - - (E4) E5 - - E8 ?
- - - - - - - - -
- -

I2: No pude

parallelion

parallelion (written in Rust) is a new competitor to GNU parallel.

It is not yet feature complete, but it is fast and has some interesting features.

It is very similar to GNU parallel, but:

  • It has a --log option, that writes job status to a file.
  • It has a --progress option, that shows a progress bar.
  • It has a --jobs option to set the number of parallel jobs.
  • It is able to read from a file.

O6: There is extra output if a job fails.

E4: The default number of parallel jobs is the number of cpu threads.

-- is needed to force args not be parsed as options:

parallelion 'echo {}' -- Runs without -v
parallelion 'echo {}' Runs with -v

The commands are run through ion shell.

Ctrl-C does not stop processing.

The --log is similar to syslog - not a table.

The progressbar is nice.

parallelion is fast: 0.1 ms/job. Similar to parallel-bash.

EXAMPLES FROM parallelion

1$ parallelion --progress 'echo {}' {1..1000}
1$ parallel --bar echo {} ::: {1..1000}
2$ parallelion --progress 'echo {}' $(seq 1 999)
2$ seq 1 999 | parallel --bar echo

https://gitlab.redox-os.org/redox-os/parallel (Last checked: 2024-08)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN Rush AND GNU Parallel

rush (https://github.com/shenwei356/rush) is written in Go and based on gargs.

Just like GNU parallel rush buffers in temporary files. But opposite GNU parallel rush does not clean up, if the process dies abnormally.

rush has some string manipulations that can be emulated by putting this into ~/.parallel/config (/ is used instead of %, and % is used instead of ^ as that is closer to bash's ${var%postfix}):

--rpl '{:} s:(\.[^/]+)*$::'
--rpl '{:%([^}]+?)} s:$$1(\.[^/]+)*$::'
--rpl '{/:%([^}]*?)} s:.*/(.*)$$1(\.[^/]+)*$:$1:'
--rpl '{/:} s:(.*/)?([^/.]+)(\.[^/]+)*$:$2:'
--rpl '{@(.*?)} /$$1/ and $_=$1;'

EXAMPLES FROM rush's WEBSITE

Here are the examples from rush's website with the equivalent command in GNU parallel.

1. Simple run, quoting is not necessary

1$ seq 1 3 | rush echo {}
1$ seq 1 3 | parallel echo {}

2. Read data from file (`-i`)

2$ rush echo {} -i data1.txt -i data2.txt
2$ cat data1.txt data2.txt | parallel echo {}

3. Keep output order (`-k`)

3$ seq 1 3 | rush 'echo {}' -k
3$ seq 1 3 | parallel -k echo {}

4. Timeout (`-t`)

4$ time seq 1 | rush 'sleep 2; echo {}' -t 1
4$ time seq 1 | parallel --timeout 1 'sleep 2; echo {}'

5. Retry (`-r`)

5$ seq 1 | rush 'python unexisted_script.py' -r 1
5$ seq 1 | parallel --retries 2 'python unexisted_script.py'

Use -u to see it is really run twice:

5$ seq 1 | parallel -u --retries 2 'python unexisted_script.py'

6. Dirname (`{/}`) and basename (`{%}`) and remove custom suffix (`{^suffix}`)

6$ echo dir/file_1.txt.gz | rush 'echo {/} {%} {^_1.txt.gz}'
6$ echo dir/file_1.txt.gz |
     parallel --plus echo {//} {/} {%_1.txt.gz}

7. Get basename, and remove last (`{.}`) or any (`{:}`) extension

7$ echo dir.d/file.txt.gz | rush 'echo {.} {:} {%.} {%:}'
7$ echo dir.d/file.txt.gz | parallel 'echo {.} {:} {/.} {/:}'

8. Job ID, combine fields index and other replacement strings

8$ echo 12 file.txt dir/s_1.fq.gz |
     rush 'echo job {#}: {2} {2.} {3%:^_1}'
8$ echo 12 file.txt dir/s_1.fq.gz |
     parallel --colsep ' ' 'echo job {#}: {2} {2.} {3/:%_1}'

9. Capture submatch using regular expression (`{@regexp}`)

9$ echo read_1.fq.gz | rush 'echo {@(.+)_\d}'
9$ echo read_1.fq.gz | parallel 'echo {@(.+)_\d}'

10. Custom field delimiter (`-d`)

10$ echo a=b=c | rush 'echo {1} {2} {3}' -d =
10$ echo a=b=c | parallel -d = echo {1} {2} {3}

11. Send multi-lines to every command (`-n`)

11$ seq 5 | rush -n 2 -k 'echo "{}"; echo'
11$ seq 5 |
      parallel -n 2 -k \
        'echo {=-1 $_=join"\n",@arg[1..$#arg] =}; echo'
11$ seq 5 | rush -n 2 -k 'echo "{}"; echo' -J ' '
11$ seq 5 | parallel -n 2 -k 'echo {}; echo'

12. Custom record delimiter (`-D`), note that empty records are not used.

12$ echo a b c d | rush -D " " -k 'echo {}'
12$ echo a b c d | parallel -d " " -k 'echo {}'
12$ echo abcd | rush -D "" -k 'echo {}'
Cannot be done by GNU Parallel
12$ cat fasta.fa
>seq1
tag
>seq2
cat
gat
>seq3
attac
a
cat
12$ cat fasta.fa | rush -D ">" \
      'echo FASTA record {#}: name: {1} sequence: {2}' -k -d "\n"
    # rush fails to join the multiline sequences
12$ cat fasta.fa | (read -n1 ignore_first_char;
      parallel -d '>' --colsep '\n' echo FASTA record {#}: \
        name: {1} sequence: '{=2 $_=join"",@arg[2..$#arg]=}'
    )

13. Assign value to variable, like `awk -v` (`-v`)

13$ seq 1 |
      rush 'echo Hello, {fname} {lname}!' -v fname=Wei -v lname=Shen
13$ seq 1 |
      parallel -N0 \
        'fname=Wei; lname=Shen; echo Hello, ${fname} ${lname}!'
13$ for var in a b; do \
13$   seq 1 3 | rush -k -v var=$var 'echo var: {var}, data: {}'; \
13$ done

In GNU parallel you would typically do:

13$ seq 1 3 | parallel -k echo var: {1}, data: {2} ::: a b :::: -

If you really want the var:

13$ seq 1 3 |
      parallel -k var={1} ';echo var: $var, data: {}' ::: a b :::: -

If you really want the for-loop:

13$ for var in a b; do
      export var;
      seq 1 3 | parallel -k 'echo var: $var, data: {}';
    done

Contrary to rush this also works if the value is complex like:

My brother's 12" records

14. Preset variable (`-v`), avoid repeatedly writing verbose replacement strings

14$ # naive way
    echo read_1.fq.gz | rush 'echo {:^_1} {:^_1}_2.fq.gz'
14$ echo read_1.fq.gz | parallel 'echo {:%_1} {:%_1}_2.fq.gz'
14$ # macro + removing suffix
    echo read_1.fq.gz |
      rush -v p='{:^_1}' 'echo {p} {p}_2.fq.gz'
14$ echo read_1.fq.gz |
      parallel 'p={:%_1}; echo $p ${p}_2.fq.gz'
14$ # macro + regular expression
    echo read_1.fq.gz | rush -v p='{@(.+?)_\d}' 'echo {p} {p}_2.fq.gz'
14$ echo read_1.fq.gz | parallel 'p={@(.+?)_\d}; echo $p ${p}_2.fq.gz'

Contrary to rush GNU parallel works with complex values:

14$ echo "My brother's 12\"read_1.fq.gz" |
      parallel 'p={@(.+?)_\d}; echo $p ${p}_2.fq.gz'

15. Interrupt jobs by `Ctrl-C`, rush will stop unfinished commands and exit.

15$ seq 1 20 | rush 'sleep 1; echo {}'
    ^C
15$ seq 1 20 | parallel 'sleep 1; echo {}'
    ^C

16. Continue/resume jobs (`-c`). When some jobs failed (by execution failure, timeout, or canceling by user with `Ctrl + C`), please switch flag `-c/--continue` on and run again, so that `rush` can save successful commands and ignore them in NEXT run.

16$ seq 1 3 | rush 'sleep {}; echo {}' -t 3 -c
    cat successful_cmds.rush
    seq 1 3 | rush 'sleep {}; echo {}' -t 3 -c
16$ seq 1 3 | parallel --joblog mylog --timeout 2 \
      'sleep {}; echo {}'
    cat mylog
    seq 1 3 | parallel --joblog mylog --retry-failed \
      'sleep {}; echo {}'

Multi-line jobs:

16$ seq 1 3 | rush 'sleep {}; echo {}; \
      echo finish {}' -t 3 -c -C finished.rush
    cat finished.rush
    seq 1 3 | rush 'sleep {}; echo {}; \
      echo finish {}' -t 3 -c -C finished.rush
16$ seq 1 3 |
      parallel --joblog mylog --timeout 2 'sleep {}; echo {}; \
        echo finish {}'
    cat mylog
    seq 1 3 |
      parallel --joblog mylog --retry-failed 'sleep {}; echo {}; \
        echo finish {}'

17. A comprehensive example: downloading 1K+ pages given by three URL list files using `phantomjs save_page.js` (some page contents are dynamically generated by Javascript, so `wget` does not work). Here I set max jobs number (`-j`) as `20`, each job has a max running time (`-t`) of `60` seconds and `3` retry changes (`-r`). Continue flag `-c` is also switched on, so we can continue unfinished jobs. Luckily, it's accomplished in one run :)

17$ for f in $(seq 2014 2016); do \
      /bin/rm -rf $f; mkdir -p $f; \
      cat $f.html.txt | rush -v d=$f -d = \
        'phantomjs save_page.js "{}" > {d}/{3}.html' \
        -j 20 -t 60 -r 3 -c; \
    done

GNU parallel can append to an existing joblog with '+':

17$ rm mylog
    for f in $(seq 2014 2016); do
      /bin/rm -rf $f; mkdir -p $f;
      cat $f.html.txt |
        parallel -j20 --timeout 60 --retries 4 --joblog +mylog \
          --colsep = \
          phantomjs save_page.js {1}={2}={3} '>' $f/{3}.html
    done

18. A bioinformatics example: mapping with `bwa`, and processing result with `samtools`:

18$ ref=ref/xxx.fa
    threads=25
    ls -d raw.cluster.clean.mapping/* \
      | rush -v ref=$ref -v j=$threads -v p='{}/{%}' \
      'bwa mem -t {j} -M -a {ref} {p}_1.fq.gz {p}_2.fq.gz >{p}.sam;\
      samtools view -bS {p}.sam > {p}.bam; \
      samtools sort -T {p}.tmp -@ {j} {p}.bam -o {p}.sorted.bam; \
      samtools index {p}.sorted.bam; \
      samtools flagstat {p}.sorted.bam > {p}.sorted.bam.flagstat; \
      /bin/rm {p}.bam {p}.sam;' \
      -j 2 --verbose -c -C mapping.rush

GNU parallel would use a function:

18$ ref=ref/xxx.fa
    export ref
    thr=25
    export thr
    bwa_sam() {
      p="$1"
      bam="$p".bam
      sam="$p".sam
      sortbam="$p".sorted.bam
      bwa mem -t $thr -M -a $ref ${p}_1.fq.gz ${p}_2.fq.gz > "$sam"
      samtools view -bS "$sam" > "$bam"
      samtools sort -T ${p}.tmp -@ $thr "$bam" -o "$sortbam"
      samtools index "$sortbam"
      samtools flagstat "$sortbam" > "$sortbam".flagstat
      /bin/rm "$bam" "$sam"
    }
    export -f bwa_sam
    ls -d raw.cluster.clean.mapping/* |
      parallel -j 2 --verbose --joblog mylog bwa_sam

Other rush features

rush has:

  • awk -v like custom defined variables (-v)

    With GNU parallel you would simply set a shell variable:

    parallel 'v={}; echo "$v"' ::: foo
echo foo | rush -v v={} 'echo {v}'

    Also rush does not like special chars. So these do not work:

    echo does not work | rush -v v=\" 'echo {v}'
echo "My  brother's  12\"  records" | rush -v v={} 'echo {v}'

    Whereas the corresponding GNU parallel version works:

    parallel 'v=\"; echo "$v"' ::: works
parallel 'v={}; echo "$v"' ::: "My  brother's  12\"  records"
  • Exit on first error(s) (-e)

    This is called --halt now,fail=1 (or shorter: --halt 2) when used with GNU parallel.

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN rush AND GNU Parallel

  • rush:

    If you have a lot of servers this way is quite tedious:

    rush -s server1 -s server2 -s server3 'command {}'

    compared to GNU parallel:

    parallel -S server1,server2,server3 'command {}'
  • Records are not settable

    This means that you cannot easily set the number of records that are sent to each command.

  • Settable records sending to every command (-n, default 1)

    This is also called -n in GNU parallel.

  • Practical replacement strings
{:} remove any extension
With GNU parallel this can be emulated by: 
parallel --plus echo '{/\..*/}' ::: foo.ext.bar.gz
{^suffix}, remove suffix
With GNU parallel this can be emulated by: 
parallel --plus echo '{%.bar.gz}' ::: foo.ext.bar.gz
{@regexp}, capture submatch using regular expression
With GNU parallel this can be emulated by: 
parallel --rpl '{@(.*?)} /$$1/ and $_=$1;' \
  echo '{@\d_(.*).gz}' ::: 1_foo.gz
{%.}, {%:}, basename without extension
With GNU parallel this can be emulated by: 
parallel echo '{= s:.*/::;s/\..*// =}' ::: dir/foo.bar.gz

And if you need it often, you define a --rpl in $HOME/.parallel/config:

--rpl '{%.} s:.*/::;s/\..*//'
--rpl '{%:} s:.*/::;s/\..*//'

Then you can use them as:

parallel echo {%.} {%:} ::: dir/foo.bar.gz
  • Preset variable (macro)

    E.g.

    echo foosuffix | rush -v p={^suffix} 'echo {p}_new_suffix'

    With GNU parallel this can be emulated by:

    echo foosuffix |
  parallel --plus 'p={%suffix}; echo ${p}_new_suffix'

    Opposite rush GNU parallel works fine if the input contains double space, ' and ":

    echo "1'6\"  foosuffix" |
  parallel --plus 'p={%suffix}; echo "${p}"_new_suffix'
  • Commands of multi-lines

    While you can use multi-lined commands in GNU parallel, to improve readability GNU parallel discourages the use of multi-line commands. In most cases it can be written as a function:

    seq 1 3 |
  parallel --timeout 2 --joblog my.log 'sleep {}; echo {}; \
    echo finish {}'

    Could be written as:

    doit() {
  sleep "$1"
  echo "$1"
  echo finish "$1"
}
export -f doit
seq 1 3 | parallel --timeout 2 --joblog my.log doit

    The failed commands can be resumed with:

    seq 1 3 |
  parallel --resume-failed --joblog my.log 'sleep {}; echo {};\
    echo finish {}'

https://github.com/shenwei356/rush (Last checked: 2017-05)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN ClusterSSH AND GNU Parallel

ClusterSSH solves a different problem than GNU parallel.

ClusterSSH opens a terminal window for each computer and using a master window you can run the same command on all the computers. This is typically used for administrating several computers that are almost identical.

GNU parallel runs the same (or different) commands with different arguments in parallel possibly using remote computers to help computing. If more than one computer is listed in -S GNU parallel may only use one of these (e.g. if there are 8 jobs to be run and one computer has 8 cores).

GNU parallel can be used as a poor-man's version of ClusterSSH:

parallel --nonall -S server-a,server-b do_stuff foo bar

https://github.com/duncs/clusterssh (Last checked: 2024-06)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN coshell AND GNU Parallel

coshell only accepts full commands on standard input. Any quoting needs to be done by the user.

Commands are run in sh so any bash/tcsh/zsh specific syntax will not work.

Output can be buffered by using -d. Output is buffered in memory, so big output can cause swapping and therefore be terrible slow or even cause out of memory.

https://github.com/gdm85/coshell (Last checked: 2019-01)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN spread AND GNU Parallel

Summary (see legend above):

- - - I4 - - I7
M1 - - - - -
O1 O2 O3 O4 O5 O6 - O8 - O10
- - - - - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

spread runs commands on all directories. It does not run jobs in parallel.

It can be emulated with GNU parallel using this Bash function:

spread() {
  _cmds() {
    perl -e '$"=" && ";print "@ARGV"' "cd {}" "$@"
  }
  parallel $(_cmds "$@")'|| echo exit status $?' ::: */
}

https://github.com/tfogo/spread (Last checked: 2024-06)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN pyargs AND GNU Parallel

pyargs deals badly with input containing spaces. It buffers stdout, but not stderr. It buffers in RAM. {} does not work as replacement string. It does not support running functions.

pyargs does not support composed commands if run with --lines, and fails on pyargs traceroute gnu.org fsf.org.

Examples

seq 5 | pyargs -P50 -L seq
seq 5 | parallel -P50 --lb seq
seq 5 | pyargs -P50 --mark -L seq
seq 5 | parallel -P50 --lb \
  --tagstring OUTPUT'[{= $_=$job->replaced() =}]' seq
# Similar, but not precisely the same
seq 5 | parallel -P50 --lb --tag seq
seq 5 | pyargs -P50  --mark command
# Somewhat longer with GNU Parallel due to the special
#   --mark formatting
cmd="$(echo "command" | parallel --shellquote)"
wrap_cmd() {
   echo "MARK $cmd $@================================" >&3
   echo "OUTPUT START[$cmd $@]:"
   eval $cmd "$@"
   echo "OUTPUT END[$cmd $@]"
}
(seq 5 | env_parallel -P2 wrap_cmd) 3>&1
# Similar, but not exactly the same
parallel seq ::: 1 2 3 4 5 6

https://github.com/robertblackwell/pyargs (Last checked: 2024-01)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN concurrently AND GNU Parallel

concurrently runs jobs in parallel.

The output is prepended with the job number, and may be incomplete:

$ concurrently 'seq 100000' | (sleep 3;wc -l)
7165

When pretty printing it caches output in memory. Output mixes by using test MIX below whether or not output is cached.

There seems to be no way of making a template command and have concurrently fill that with different args. The full commands must be given on the command line.

There is also no way of controlling how many jobs should be run in parallel at a time - i.e. "number of jobslots". Instead all jobs are simply started in parallel.

https://github.com/kimmobrunfeldt/concurrently (Last checked: 2019-01)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN map(soveran) AND GNU Parallel

map does not run jobs in parallel by default. The README suggests using:

... | map t 'sleep $t && say done &'

But this fails if more jobs are run in parallel than the number of available processes. Since there is no support for parallelization in map itself, the output also mixes:

seq 10 | map i 'echo start-$i && sleep 0.$i && echo end-$i &'

The major difference is that GNU parallel is built for parallelization and map is not. So GNU parallel has lots of ways of dealing with the issues that parallelization raises:

  • Keep the number of processes manageable
  • Make sure output does not mix
  • Make Ctrl-C kill all running processes

EXAMPLES FROM maps WEBSITE

Here are the 5 examples converted to GNU Parallel:

1$ ls *.c | map f 'foo $f'
1$ ls *.c | parallel foo
2$ ls *.c | map f 'foo $f; bar $f'
2$ ls *.c | parallel 'foo {}; bar {}'
3$ cat urls | map u 'curl -O $u'
3$ cat urls | parallel curl -O
4$ printf "1\n1\n1\n" | map t 'sleep $t && say done'
4$ printf "1\n1\n1\n" | parallel 'sleep {} && say done'
4$ parallel 'sleep {} && say done' ::: 1 1 1
5$ printf "1\n1\n1\n" | map t 'sleep $t && say done &'
5$ printf "1\n1\n1\n" | parallel -j0 'sleep {} && say done'
5$ parallel -j0 'sleep {} && say done' ::: 1 1 1

https://github.com/soveran/map (Last checked: 2019-01)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN loop AND GNU Parallel

loop mixes stdout and stderr:

loop 'ls /no-such-file' >/dev/null

loop's replacement string $ITEM does not quote strings:

echo 'two  spaces' | loop 'echo $ITEM'

loop cannot run functions:

myfunc() { echo joe; }
export -f myfunc
loop 'myfunc this fails'

EXAMPLES FROM loop's WEBSITE

Some of the examples from https://github.com/Miserlou/Loop/ can be emulated with GNU parallel:

# A couple of functions will make the code easier to read
$ loopy() {
    yes | parallel -uN0 -j1 "$@"
  }
$ export -f loopy
$ time_out() {
    parallel -uN0 -q --timeout "$@" ::: 1
  }
$ match() {
    perl -0777 -ne 'grep /'"$1"'/,$_ and print or exit 1'
  }
$ expor
t -f match $ loop 'ls' --every 10s $ loopy --delay 10s ls $ loop 'touch $COUNT.txt' --count-by 5 $ loopy touch '{= $_=seq()*5 =}'.txt $ loop --until-contains 200 -- \ ./get_response_code.sh --site mysite.biz $ loopy --halt now,success=1 \ './get_response_code.sh --site mysite.biz | match 200' $ loop './poke_server' --for-duration 8h $ time_out 8h loopy ./poke_server $ loop './poke_server' --until-success $ loopy --halt now,success=1 ./poke_server $ cat files_to_create.txt | loop 'touch $ITEM' $ cat files_to_create.txt | parallel touch {} $ loop 'ls' --for-duration 10min --summary # --joblog es algo más verboso que --summary $ time_out 10m loopy --joblog my.log ./poke_server; cat my.log $ loop 'echo hello' $ loopy echo hello $ loop 'echo $COUNT' # GNU Parallel cuenta desde 1 $ loopy echo {#} # Se puede forzar el conteo desde 0 $ loopy echo '{= $_=seq()-1 =}' $ loop 'echo $COUNT' --count-by 2 $ loopy echo '{= $_=2*(seq()-1) =}' $ loop 'echo $COUNT' --count-by 2 --offset 10 $ loopy echo '{= $_=10+2*(seq()-1) =}' $ loop 'echo $COUNT' --count-by 1.1 # GNU Parallel redondea 3.3000000000000003 a 3.3 $ loopy echo '{= $_=1.1*(seq()-1) =}' $ loop 'echo $COUNT $ACTUALCOUNT' --count-by 2 $ loopy echo '{= $_=2*(seq()-1) =} {#}' $ loop 'echo $COUNT' --num 3 --summary # --joblog es algo más verboso que --summary $ seq 3 | parallel --joblog my.log echo; cat my.log $ loop 'ls -foobarbatz' --num 3 --summary # --joblog es algo más verboso que --summary $ seq 3 | parallel --joblog my.log -N0 ls -foobarbatz; cat my.log $ loop 'echo $COUNT' --count-by 2 --num 50 --only-last # Se puede emular ejecutando 2 trabajos $ seq 49 | parallel echo '{= $_=2*(seq()-1) =}' >/dev/null $ echo 50| parallel echo '{= $_=2*(seq()-1) =}' $ loop 'date' --every 5s $ loopy --delay 5s date $ loop 'date' --for-duration 8s --every 2s $ time_out 8s loopy --delay 2s date $ loop 'date -u' --until-time '2018-05-25 20:50:00' --every 5s $ seconds=$((`date -d 2019-05-25T20:50:00 +%s` - `date +%s`))s $ time_out $seconds loopy --delay 5s date -u $ loop 'echo $RANDOM' --until-contains "666" $ loopy --halt now,success=1 'echo $RANDOM | match 666' $ loop 'if (( RANDOM % 2 )); then (echo "TRUE"; true); else (echo "FALSE"; false); fi' --until-success $ loopy --halt now,success=1 'if (( $RANDOM % 2 )); then (echo "TRUE"; true); else (echo "FALSE"; false); fi' $ loop 'if (( RANDOM % 2 )); then (echo "TRUE"; true); else (echo "FALSE"; false); fi' --until-error $ loopy --halt now,fail=1 'if (( $RANDOM % 2 )); then (echo "TRUE"; true); else (echo "FALSE"; false); fi' $ loop 'date' --until-match "(\d{4})" $ loopy --halt now,success=1 'date | match [0-9][0-9][0-9][0-9]' $ loop 'echo $ITEM' --for red,green,blue $ parallel echo ::: red green blue $ cat /tmp/my-list-of-files-to-create.txt | loop 'touch $ITEM' $ cat /tmp/my-list-of-files-to-create.txt | parallel touch $ ls | loop 'cp $ITEM $ITEM.bak'; ls $ ls | parallel cp {} {}.bak; ls $ loop 'echo $ITEM | tr a-z A-Z' -i $ parallel 'echo {} | tr a-z A-Z' # O más eficientemente: $ parallel --pipe tr a-z A-Z $ loop 'echo $ITEM' --for "`ls`" $ parallel echo {} ::: "`ls`" $ ls | loop './my_program $ITEM' --until-success; $ ls | parallel --halt now,success=1 ./my_program {} $ ls | loop './my_program $ITEM' --until-fail; $ ls | parallel --halt now,fail=1 ./my_program {} $ ./deploy.sh; loop 'curl -sw "%{http_code}" http://coolwebsite.biz' \ --every 5s --until-contains 200; ./announce_to_slack.sh $ ./deploy.sh; loopy --delay 5s --halt now,success=1 \ 'curl -sw "%{http_code}" http://coolwebsite.biz | match 200'; ./announce_to_slack.sh $ loop "ping -c 1 mysite.com" --until-success; ./do_next_thing $ loopy --halt now,success=1 ping -c 1 mysite.com; ./do_next_thing $ ./create_big_file -o my_big_file.bin; loop 'ls' --until-contains 'my_big_file.bin'; ./upload_big_file my_big_file.bin # inotifywait es una mejor herramienta para detectar cambios en el sistema de archivos. # Incluso puede asegurarse de que el archivo esté completo # para que no subas un archivo incompleto $ inotifywait -qmre MOVED_TO -e CLOSE_WRITE --format %w%f . | grep my_big_file.bin $ ls | loop 'cp $ITEM $ITEM.bak' $ ls | parallel cp {} {}.bak $ loop './do_thing.sh' --every 15s --until-success --num 5 $ parallel --retries 5 --delay 15s ::: ./do_thing.sh

https://github.com/Miserlou/Loop/ (Última comprobación: 2018-10)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE lorikeet Y GNU Parallel

lorikeet puede ejecutar trabajos en paralelo. Lo hace basándose en un grafo de dependencias descrito en un archivo, por lo que es similar a make.

https://github.com/cetra3/lorikeet (Última comprobación: 2018-10)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE spp Y GNU Parallel

spp puede ejecutar trabajos en paralelo. spp no utiliza una plantilla de comando para generar los trabajos, sino que requiere que los trabajos estén en un archivo. La salida de los trabajos se mezcla.

https://github.com/john01dav/spp (Última comprobación: 2024-06)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE paral Y GNU Parallel

paral imprime mucha información de estado y almacena la salida de los comandos ejecutados en archivos. Esto significa que no se puede utilizar en medio de una tubería como esta:

paral "command 1" "command 2" "command 3" | wc

En su lugar, pone la salida en archivos con nombres como out_#_comando.out.log. Para obtener un comportamiento muy similar con GNU parallel, utiliza --results 'out_{#}_{=s/[^\sa-z_0-9]//g;s/\s+/_/g=}.log' --eta

paral solo toma argumentos en la línea de comandos y cada argumento debe ser un comando completo. Por lo tanto, no utiliza plantillas de comandos.

Esto limita cuántos trabajos puede ejecutar en total, ya que todos deben caber en una única línea de comandos.

paral no tiene soporte para ejecutar trabajos de forma remota.

EJEMPLOS DEL README.markdown

Los ejemplos de README.markdown y el comando correspondiente ejecutado con GNU parallel (se omite --results 'out_{#}_{=s/[^\sa-z_0-9]//g;s/\s+/_/g=}.log' --eta en el comando GNU parallel):

1$ paral "command 1" "command 2 --flag" "command arg1 arg2"
1$ parallel ::: "command 1" "command 2 --flag" "command arg1 arg2"
2$ paral "sleep 1 && echo c1" "sleep 2 && echo c2" \
     "sleep 3 && echo c3" "sleep 4 && echo c4"  "sleep 5 && echo c5"
2$ parallel ::: "sleep 1 && echo c1" "sleep 2 && echo c2" \
     "sleep 3 && echo c3" "sleep 4 && echo c4"  "sleep 5 && echo c5"
   # O más corto:
   parallel "sleep {} && echo c{}" ::: {1..5}
3$ paral -n=0 "sleep 5 && echo c5" "sleep 4 && echo c4" \
     "sleep 3 && echo c3" "sleep 2 && echo c2" "sleep 1 && echo c1"
3$ parallel ::: "sleep 5 && echo c5" "sleep 4 && echo c4" \
     "sleep 3 && echo c3" "sleep 2 && echo c2" "sleep 1 && echo c1"
   # O más corto:
   parallel -j0 "sleep {} && echo c{}" ::: 5 4 3 2 1
4$ paral -n=1 "sleep 5 && echo c5" "sleep 4 && echo c4" \
     "sleep 3 && echo c3" "sleep 2 && echo c2" "sleep 1 && echo c1"
4$ parallel -j1 "sleep {} && echo c{}" ::: 5 4 3 2 1
5$ paral -n=2 "sleep 5 && echo c5" "sleep 4 && echo c4" \
     "sleep 3 && echo c3" "sleep 2 && echo c2" "sleep 1 && echo c1"
5$ parallel -j2 "sleep {} && echo c{}" ::: 5 4 3 2 1
6$ paral -n=5 "sleep 5 && echo c5" "sleep 4 && echo c4" \
     "sleep 3 && echo c3" "sleep 2 && echo c2" "sleep 1 && echo c1"
6$ parallel -j5 "sleep {} && echo c{}" ::: 5 4 3 2 1
7$ paral -n=1 "echo a && sleep 0.5 && echo b && sleep 0.5 && \
     echo c && sleep 0.5 && echo d && sleep 0.5 && \
     echo e && sleep 0.5 && echo f && sleep 0.5 && \
     echo g && sleep 0.5 && echo h"
7$ parallel ::: "echo a && sleep 0.5 && echo b && sleep 0.5 && \
     echo c && sleep 0.5 && echo d && sleep 0.5 && \
     echo e && sleep 0.5 && echo f && sleep 0.5 && \
     echo g && sleep 0.5 && echo h"

https://github.com/amattn/paral (Última comprobación: 2024-06)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE concurr Y GNU Parallel

concurr está diseñado para ejecutar trabajos en paralelo utilizando un modelo cliente/servidor.

EJEMPLOS DE README.md

Los ejemplos de README.md:

1$ concurr 'echo job {#} on slot {%}: {}' : arg1 arg2 arg3 arg4
1$ parallel 'echo job {#} on slot {%}: {}' ::: arg1 arg2 arg3 arg4
2$ concurr 'echo job {#} on slot {%}: {}' :: file1 file2 file3
2$ parallel 'echo job {#} on slot {%}: {}' :::: file1 file2 file3
3$ concurr 'echo {}' < input_file
3$ parallel 'echo {}' < input_file
4$ cat file | concurr 'echo {}'
4$ cat file | parallel 'echo {}'

concurr maneja mal los archivos de entrada vacíos y la salida mayor de 64 KB.

https://github.com/mmstick/concurr (Última comprobación: 2024-01)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE lesser-parallel Y GNU Parallel

lesser-parallel es la inspiración para parallel --embed. Tanto lesser-parallel como parallel --embed definen funciones de bash que pueden incluirse como parte de un script de bash para ejecutar trabajos en paralelo.

lesser-parallel implementa algunas de las cadenas de reemplazo, pero apenas algunas opciones, mientras que parallel --embed te brinda la experiencia completa de GNU parallel.

https://github.com/kou1okada/lesser-parallel (L

Comprobado por última vez: 2024-06)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE npm-parallel Y GNU Parallel

npm-parallel puede ejecutar tareas de npm en paralelo.

No hay ejemplos y muy poca documentación, por lo que es difícil compararlo con GNU parallel.

https://github.com/spion/npm-parallel (Comprobado por última vez: 2024-06)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE machma Y GNU Parallel

machma ejecuta tareas en paralelo. Proporciona salida con marca de tiempo. Almacena en búfer en RAM.

EJEMPLOS DE README.md

Los ejemplos de README.md:

1$ # Poner un atajo para la marca de tiempo en la configuración para los ejemplos
   echo '--rpl '\
     \''{time} $_=::strftime("%Y-%m-%d %H:%M:%S",localtime())'\' \
     > ~/.parallel/machma
   echo '--line-buffer --tagstring "{#} {time} {}"' \
     >> ~/.parallel/machma
2$ find . -iname '*.jpg' |
     machma --  mogrify -resize 1200x1200 -filter Lanczos {}
   find . -iname '*.jpg' |
     parallel --bar -Jmachma mogrify -resize 1200x1200 \
       -filter Lanczos {}
3$ cat /tmp/ips | machma -p 2 -- ping -c 2 -q {}
3$ cat /tmp/ips | parallel -j2 -Jmachma ping -c 2 -q {}
4$ cat /tmp/ips |
     machma -- sh -c 'ping -c 2 -q $0 > /dev/null && echo alive' {}
4$ cat /tmp/ips |
     parallel -Jmachma 'ping -c 2 -q {} > /dev/null && echo alive'
5$ find . -iname '*.jpg' |
     machma --timeout 5s -- mogrify -resize 1200x1200 \
       -filter Lanczos {}
5$ find . -iname '*.jpg' |
     parallel --timeout 5s --bar mogrify -resize 1200x1200 \
       -filter Lanczos {}
6$ find . -iname '*.jpg' -print0 |
     machma --null --  mogrify -resize 1200x1200 -filter Lanczos {}
6$ find . -iname '*.jpg' -print0 |
     parallel --null --bar mogrify -resize 1200x1200 \
       -filter Lanczos {}

https://github.com/fd0/machma (Comprobado por última vez: 2019-06)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE interlace Y GNU Parallel

Resumen (ver leyenda arriba):

- I2 I3 I4 - - -
M1 - M3 - - M6
- O2 O3 - - - - x x
E1 E2 - - - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

interlace está construido para análisis de red para ejecutar herramientas de red en paralelo.

interlace no almacena en búfer la salida, por lo que la salida de diferentes trabajos se mezcla.

La sobrecarga para cada destino es O(n*n), por lo que con 1000 destinos se vuelve muy lento con una sobrecarga del orden de 500 ms/destino.

EJEMPLOS DEL SITIO WEB DE interlace

Usando prips, la mayoría de los ejemplos de https://github.com/codingo/Interlace se pueden ejecutar con GNU parallel:

Bloqueador

commands.txt:
  mkdir -p _output_/_target_/scans/
  _blocker_
  nmap _target_ -oA _output_/_target_/scans/_target_-nmap
interlace -tL ./targets.txt -cL commands.txt -o $output
parallel -a targets.txt \
  mkdir -p $output/{}/scans/\; nmap {} -oA $output/{}/scans/{}-nmap

Bloques

commands.txt:
  _block:nmap_
  mkdir -p _target_/output/scans/
  nmap _target_ -oN _target_/output/scans/_target_-nmap
  _block:nmap_
  nikto --host _target_
interlace -tL ./targets.txt -cL commands.txt
_nmap() {
  mkdir -p $1/output/scans/
  nmap $1 -oN $1/output/scans/$1-nmap
}
export -f _nmap
parallel ::: _nmap "nikto --host" :::: targets.txt

Ejecutar Nikto en varios sitios

interlace -tL ./targets.txt -threads 5 \
  -c "nikto --host _target_ > ./_target_-nikto.txt" -v
parallel -a targets.txt -P5 nikto --host {} \> ./{}_-nikto.txt

Ejecutar Nikto en varios sitios y puertos

interlace -tL ./targets.txt -threads 5 -c \
  "nikto --host _target_:_port_ > ./_target_-_port_-nikto.txt" \
  -p 80,443 -v
parallel -P5 nikto --host {1}:{2} \> ./{1}-{2}-nikto.txt \
  :::: targets.txt ::: 80 443

Ejecutar una lista de comandos contra hosts de destino

commands.txt:
  nikto --host _target_:_port_ > _output_/_target_-nikto.txt
  sslscan _target_:_port_ >  _output_/_target_-sslscan.txt
  testssl.sh _target_:_port_ > _output_/_target_-testssl.txt
interlace -t example.com -o ~/Engagements/example/ \
  -cL ./commands.txt -p 80,443
parallel --results ~/Engagements/example/{2}:{3}{1} {1} {2}:{3} \
  ::: "nikto --host" sslscan testssl.sh ::: example.com ::: 80 443

Notación CIDR con una aplicación que no la soporta

interlace -t 192.168.12.0/24 -c "vhostscan _target_ \
  -oN _output_/_target_-vhosts.txt" -o ~/scans/ -threads 50
prips 192.168.12.0/24 |
  parallel -P50 vhostscan {} -oN ~/scans/{}-vhosts.txt

Notación glob con una aplicación que no la soporta

interlace -t 192.168.12.* -c "vhostscan _target_ \
  -oN _output_/_target_-vhosts.txt" -o ~/scans/ -threads 50
# Glob no es compatible con prips
prips 192.168.12.0/24 |
  parallel -P50 vhostscan {} -oN ~/scans/{}-vhosts.txt

Notación de guion (-) con una aplicación que no la soporta

interlace -t 192.168.12.1-15 -c \
  "vhostscan _target_ -oN _output_/_target_-vhosts.txt" \
  -o ~/scans/ -threads 50
# La notación de guion no es compatible con prips
prips 192.168.12.1 192.168.12.15 |
  parallel -P50 vhostscan {} -oN ~/scans/{}-vhosts.txt

Soporte de hilos para una aplicación que no lo soporta

interlace -tL ./target-list.txt -c \
  "vhostscan -t _target_ -oN _output_/_target_-vhosts.txt" \
  -o ~/scans/ -threads 50
cat ./target-list.txt |
  parallel -P50 vhostscan -t {} -oN ~/scans/{}-vhosts.txt

alternativamente

./vhosts-commands.txt:
  vhostscan -t $target -oN _output_/_target_-vhosts.txt
interlace -cL ./vhosts-commands.txt -tL ./target-list.txt \
  -threads 50 -o ~/scans
./vhosts-commands.txt:
  vhostscan -t "$1" -oN "$2"
parallel -P50 ./vhosts-commands.txt {} ~/scans/{}-vhosts.txt \
  :::: ./target-list.txt

Exclusiones

interlace -t 192.168.12.0/24 -e 192.168.12.0/26 -c \
  "vhostscan _target_ -oN _output_/_target_-vhosts.txt" \
  -o ~/scans/ -threads 50
prips 192.168.12.0/24 | grep -xv -Ff <(prips 192.168.12.0/26) |
  parallel -P50 vhostscan {} -oN ~/scans/{}-vhosts.txt

Ejecutar Nikto usando múltiples proxies

interlace -tL ./targets.txt -pL ./proxies.txt -threads 5 -c \
  "nikto --host _target_:_port_ -useproxy _proxy_ > \
   ./_target_-_port_-nikto.txt" -p 80,443 -v
parallel -j5 \
  "nikto --host {1}:{2} -useproxy {3} > ./{1}-{2}-nikto.txt" \
  :::: ./targets.txt ::: 80 443 :::: ./proxies.txt

https://github.com/codingo/Interlace (Comprobado por última vez: 2019-09)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE otonvm Parallel Y GNU Parallel

No he podido ejecutar el código en absoluto. Parece incompleto.

https://github.com/otonvm/Parallel (Comprobado por última vez: 2024-06)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE k-bx par Y GNU Parallel

par requiere Haskell para funcionar. Esto limita el número de plataformas en las que puede funcionar.

par realiza un búfer de línea en memoria. El uso de memoria es 3 veces la línea más larga (en comparación con 1 vez para parallel --lb). Los comandos deben proporcionarse como argumentos. No hay plantilla.

Estos son los ejemplos de https://github.com/k-bx/par con el comando GNU parallel correspondiente.

par "echo foo; sleep 1; echo foo; sleep 1; echo foo" \
    "echo bar; sleep 1; echo bar; sleep 1; echo bar" && echo "success"
parallel --lb ::: "echo foo; sleep 1; echo foo; sleep 1; echo foo" \
    "echo bar; sleep 1; echo bar; sleep 1; echo bar" && echo "success"
par "echo foo; sleep 1; foofoo" \
    "echo bar; sleep 1; echo bar; sleep 1; echo bar" && echo "success"
parallel --lb --halt 1 ::: "echo foo; sleep 1; foofoo" \
    "echo bar; sleep 1; echo bar; sleep 1; echo bar" && echo "success"
par "PARPREFIX=[fooechoer] echo foo" "PARPREFIX=[bar] echo bar"
parallel --lb --colsep , --tagstring {1} {2} \
  ::: "[fooechoer],echo foo" "[bar],echo bar"
par --succeed "foo" "bar" && echo 'wow'
parallel "foo" "bar"; true && echo 'wow'

https://github.com/k-bx/par (Comprobado por última vez: 2019-02)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE parallelshell Y GNU Parallel

parallelshell no permite comandos compuestos:

# Esto no funciona
parallelshell 'echo foo;echo bar' 'echo baz;echo quuz'

En su lugar, tienes que envolverlo en un shell:

parallelshell 'sh -c "echo foo;echo bar"' 'sh -c "echo baz;echo quuz"'

Almacena en búfer la salida en RAM. Todos los comandos deben proporcionarse en la línea de comandos y todos los comandos se inician en paralelo al mismo tiempo. Esto provocará que el sistema se congele si hay tantos trabajos que no hay suficiente memoria para ejecutarlos todos al mismo tiempo.

https://github.com/keithamus/parallelshell (Comprobado por última vez: 2019-02)

https://github.com/darkguy2008/parallelshell (Comprobado por última vez: 2019-03)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE shell-executor Y GNU Parallel

shell-executor no permite comandos compuestos:

# Esto no funciona
sx 'echo foo;echo bar' 'echo baz;echo quuz'

En su lugar, tienes que envolverlo en un shell:

sx 'sh -c "echo foo;echo bar"' 'sh -c "echo baz;echo quuz"'

Almacena en búfer la salida en RAM. Todos los comandos deben proporcionarse en la línea de comandos y todos los comandos se inician en paralelo al mismo tiempo. Esto provocará que

.TH GNU Parallel 1 "" "GNU Parallel 20240622" "GNU Parallel" .SH NAME parallel \- execute jobs in parallel .SH SYNOPSIS .B parallel [OPTION]... [COMMAND] .br .B parallel --citation [FILE]... .br .B parallel --citation --all .SH DESCRIPTION This manual page briefly summarizes the differences between GNU .B parallel and similar tools. .PP A legend for the symbols used: .TP .B I Input: .BR .I1 Input is read from stdin. .BR .I2 Input is read from arguments. .BR .I3 Input is read from file. .BR .I4 Input is read from find. .BR .I5 Input is read from seq. .BR .I6 Input is read from env. .BR .I7 Input is read from the command line. .TP .B M Output modifiers: .BR .M1 Output is prepended with input. .BR .M2 Output is prepended with job sequence number. .BR .M3 Output is prepended with job number. .BR .M4 Output is prepended with job name. .BR .M5 Output is prepended with job size. .BR .M6 Output is prepended with job CPU time. .TP .B O Output: .BR .O1 Standard output is printed to standard output. .BR .O2 Standard output is buffered in RAM. .BR .O3 Standard output is buffered to disk. .BR .O4 Standard output is merged with standard error. .BR .O5 Standard output is sent to stdout. .BR .O6 Standard output is sent to a file. .BR .O7 Standard output is stored per job. .BR .O8 Standard output is stored in a temp directory. .TP .B E Error handling: .BR .E1 Standard error is merged with standard output. .BR .E2 Standard error is sent to stdout. .BR .E3 Standard error is sent to a file. .BR .E4 Standard error is logged. .BR .E5 Error handling is configurable. .BR .E6 Jobs that fail are re-run. .BR .E7 Jobs that fail are removed. .TP .B X Exit codes: .BR .X1 Exit code is 0 if all jobs succeed. .BR .X2 Exit code is the exit code of the first job that failed. .TP .B S Shell features: .BR .S1 Composed commands are supported. .BR .S2 Functions are supported. .BR .S3 Pipes are supported. .BR .S4 Redirections are supported. .BR .S5 Subshells are supported. .BR .S6 Variable substitution is supported. .TP .B C Configuration: .BR .C1 Configuration is possible. .TP .B T Terminal: .BR .T1 Terminal control is supported. .TP .B R Resource management: .BR .R1 Resource management is supported. .TP .B L Logging: .BR .L1 Logging is supported. .TP .B J Job management: .BR .J1 Job management is supported. .TP .B F File management: .BR .F1 File management is supported. .TP .B P Progress: .BR .P1 Progress is shown. .TP .B A Arguments: .BR .A1 Arguments are read from command line. .BR .A2 Arguments are read from file. .BR .A3 Arguments are read from stdin. .TP .B V Virtualization: .BR .V1 Virtualization is supported. .TP .B Z Zero termination: .BR .Z1 Zero termination is supported. .SS DIFFERENCES BETWEEN xargs AND GNU Parallel .PP Summary (see legend above): .TP .B I1 I2 I3 - I5 - - .TP .B M1 M2 M3 - - M6 .TP .B O1 O2 - O4 O5 O6 - .TP .B E1 - E3 - - E7 .TP .B X1 X2 - - - - - - .TP .B S1 S2 S3 S4 S5 S6 .TP .B C1 - - - - - .TP .B - - - - - - .TP .B R1 - - - - - .TP .B L1 - - - - - .TP .B J1 - - - - - .TP .B F1 - - - - - .TP .B P1 - - - - - .TP .B A1 A2 A3 - - - .TP .B - - - - - - .TP .B V1 - - - - - .TP .B Z1 - - - - - .PP GNU .B parallel can run the same jobs in parallel as GNU .B xargs . .PP .I xargs reads items from standard input, delimited by blanks (which can be quoted) or newlines, chooses items to load into the command line until the command line is full, and then executes the command. .PP GNU .B parallel is more flexible. It can also read input from arguments, a file, or .I find . It supports more delimiters and can execute commands in parallel based on a list of arguments. It also supports more shell features such as pipes, redirections, and composed commands. .PP GNU .B parallel also handles arguments with spaces in them correctly. This is a common problem with .I xargs . .PP The .B --rpl option in GNU .B parallel allows for replacement strings, similar to what .I xargs -I does, but with more flexibility. .PP Example: .PP .I xargs .IP .nf echo a b | xargs -n 1 echo .fi .IP .nf echo a b | parallel -n 1 echo .fi .PP .I xargs .IP .nf echo "a b" | xargs -n 1 echo .fi .IP .nf echo "a b" | parallel -n 1 echo .fi .PP .I xargs .IP .nf ls -d *.txt | xargs -n 1 echo .fi .IP .nf ls -d *.txt | parallel -n 1 echo .fi .PP .I xargs .IP .nf echo "a b" | xargs -d' ' -n 1 echo .fi .IP .nf echo "a b" | parallel -d' ' -n 1 echo .fi .PP .I xargs .IP .nf ls -d *.txt | xargs -I{} echo {} .fi .IP .nf ls -d *.txt | parallel echo {} .fi .PP GNU .B parallel can also build argument lists from file which .I xargs cannot do. .PP .I xargs will also crash the system if there are so many jobs that there is not enough memory to run them all at the same time. .PP https://github.com/royriojas/shell-executor (Last checked: 2024-06) .SS DIFFERENCES BETWEEN non-GNU par AND GNU Parallel .PP .B par buffers in memory to avoid mixing of jobs. It takes 1s per 1 million output lines. .PP .B par needs to have all commands before starting the first job. The jobs are read from stdin (standard input) so any quoting will have to be done by the user. .PP Stdout (standard output) is prepended with o:. Stderr (standard error) is sendt to stdout (standard output) and prepended with e:. .PP For short jobs with little output .B par is 20% faster than GNU .B parallel and 60% slower than .B xargs . .PP https://github.com/UnixJunkie/PAR .PP https://savannah.nongnu.org/projects/par (Last checked: 2019-02) .SS DIFFERENCES BETWEEN fd AND GNU Parallel .PP .B fd does not support composed commands, so commands must be wrapped in .B sh -c . .PP It buffers output in RAM. .PP It only takes file names from the filesystem as input (similar to .B find ). .PP https://github.com/sharkdp/fd (Last checked: 2019-02) .SS DIFFERENCES BETWEEN lateral AND GNU Parallel .PP .B lateral is very similar to .B sem : It takes a single command and runs it in the background. The design means that output from parallel running jobs may mix. If it dies unexpectly it leaves a socket in ~/.lateral/socket.PID. .PP .B lateral deals badly with too long command lines. This makes the .B lateral server crash: .TP .nf lateral run echo `seq 100000| head -c 1000k` .fi .PP Any options will be read by .B lateral so this does not work ( .B lateral interprets the .B -l ): .TP .nf lateral run ls -l .fi .PP Composed commands do not work: .TP .nf lateral run pwd ';' ls .fi .PP Functions do not work: .TP .nf myfunc() { echo a; } export -f myfunc lateral run myfunc .fi .PP Running .B emacs in the terminal causes the parent shell to die: .TP .nf echo '#!/bin/bash' > mycmd echo emacs -nw >> mycmd chmod +x mycmd lateral start lateral run ./mycmd .fi .PP Here are the examples from https://github.com/akramer/lateral with the corresponding GNU .B sem and GNU .B parallel commands: .TP .nf 1$ lateral start for i in $(cat /tmp/names); do lateral run -- some_command $i done lateral wait 1$ for i in $(cat /tmp/names); do sem some_command $i done sem --wait 1$ parallel some_command :::: /tmp/names 2$ lateral start for i in $(seq 1 100); do lateral run -- my_slow_command < workfile$i > /tmp/logfile$i done lateral wait 2$ for i in $(seq 1 100); do sem my_slow_command < workfile$i > /tmp/logfile$i done sem --wait 2$ parallel 'my_slow_command < workfile{} > /tmp/logfile{}' \ ::: {1..100} 3$ lateral start -p 0 # yup, it will just queue tasks for i in $(seq 1 100); do lateral run -- command_still_outputs_but_wont_spam inputfile$i done # command output spam can commence lateral config -p 10; lateral wait 3$ for i in $(seq 1 100); do echo "command inputfile$i" >> joblist done parallel -j 10 :::: joblist 3$ echo 1 > /tmp/njobs parallel -j /tmp/njobs command inputfile{} \ ::: {1..100} & echo 10 >/tmp/njobs wait .fi .PP https://github.com/akramer/lateral (Last checked: 2024-06) .SS DIFFERENCES BETWEEN with-this AND GNU Parallel .PP The examples from https://github.com/amritb/with-this.git and the corresponding GNU .B parallel command: .TP .nf with -v "$(cat myurls.txt)" "curl -L this" parallel curl -L ::: myurls.txt with -v "$(cat myregions.txt)" \ "aws --region=this ec2 describe-instance-status" parallel aws --region={} ec2 describe-instance-status \ :::: myregions.txt with -v "$(ls)" "kubectl --kubeconfig=this get pods" ls | parallel kubectl --kubeconfig={} get pods with -v "$(ls | grep config)" "kubectl --kubeconfig=this get pods" ls | grep config | parallel kubectl --kubeconfig={} get pods with -v "$(echo {1..10})" "echo 123" parallel -N0 echo 123 ::: {1..10} .fi .PP Stderr is merged with stdout. .B with-this buffers in RAM. It uses 3x the output size, so you cannot have output larger than 1/3rd the amount of RAM. The input values cannot contain spaces. Composed commands do not work. .PP .B with-this gives some additional information, so the output has to be cleaned before piping it to the next command. .PP https://github.com/amritb/with-this.git (Last checked: 2024-06) .SS DIFFERENCES BETWEEN Tollef's parallel (moreutils) AND GNU Parallel .PP Summary (see legend above): .TP .B - - - I4 - - I7 .TP .B - - M3 - - M6 .TP .B - O2 O3 - O5 O6 - x x .TP .B E1 - - - - - E7 .TP .B - x x x x x x x x .TP .B - - .PP .I EXAMPLES FROM Tollef's parallel MANUAL .PP .B Tollef parallel sh -c "echo hi; sleep 2; echo bye" -- 1 2 3 .PP .B GNU parallel "echo hi; sleep 2; echo bye" ::: 1 2 3 .PP .B Tollef parallel -j 3 ufraw -o processed -- *.NEF .PP .B GNU parallel -j 3 ufraw -o processed ::: *.NEF .PP .B Tollef parallel -j 3 -- ls df "echo hi" .PP .B GNU parallel -j 3 ::: ls df "echo hi" .PP (Last checked: 2019-08) .SS DIFFERENCES BETWEEN rargs AND GNU Parallel .PP Summary (see legend above): .TP .B I1 - - - - - I7 .TP .B - - M3 M4 - - .TP .B - O2 O3 - O5 O6 - O8 - .TP .B E1 - - E4 - - - .TP .B - - - - - - - - - .TP .B - - .PP .B rargs has elegant ways of doing named regexp capture and field ranges. .PP With GNU .B parallel you can use .B --rpl to get a similar functionality as regexp capture gives, and use .B join and .BR .I @arg .BR to get the field ranges. But the syntax is longer. This: .TP .nf --rpl '{r(\d+)..(\d+)} $_=join("..",@arg[$$1..$$2])' .fi .PP would make it possible to use: .TP .nf {1r3..6} .fi .PP for field 3..6. .PP For full support of {n..m:s} including negative numbers use a dynamic replacement string like this: .TP .nf PARALLEL=--rpl\ \''{r((-?\d+)?)..((-?\d+)?)((:([^}]*))?)} $a = defined $$2 ? $$2 < 0 ? 1+$#arg+$$2 : $$2 : 1; $b = defined $$4 ? $$4 < 0 ? 1+$#arg+$$4 : $$4 : $#arg+1; $s = defined $$6 ? $$7 : " "; $_ = join $s,@arg[$a..$b]'\' export PARALLEL .fi .PP You can then do: .TP .nf head /etc/passwd | parallel --colsep : echo ..={1r..} ..3={1r..3} \ 4..={1r4..} 2..4={1r2..4} 3..3={1r3..3} ..3:-={1r..3:-} \ ..3:/={1r..3:/} -1={-1} -5={-5} -6={-6} -3..={1r-3..} .fi .PP .I EXAMPLES FROM rargs MANUAL .PP .TP .nf 1$ ls *.bak | rargs -p '(.*)\.bak' mv {0} {1} 1$ ls *.bak | parallel mv {} {.} 2$ cat download-list.csv | rargs -p '(?P.*),(?P.*)' wget {url} -O {filename} 2$ cat download-list.csv | parallel --csv wget {1} -O {2} # or use regexps: 2$ cat download-list.csv | parallel --rpl '{url} s/,.*//' --rpl '{filename} s/.*?,//' \ wget {url} -O {filename} 3$ cat /etc/passwd | rargs -d: echo -e 'id: "{1}"\t name: "{5}"\t rest: "{6..::}"' 3$ cat /etc/passwd | parallel -q --colsep : \ echo -e 'id: "{1}"\t name: "{5}"\t rest: "{=6 $_=join(":",@arg[6..$#arg])=}"' .fi .PP https://github.com/lotabout/rargs (Last checked: 2020-01) .SS DIFFERENCES BETWEEN threader AND GNU Parallel .PP Summary (see legend above): .TP .B I1 - - - - - - .TP .B M1 - M3 - - M6 .TP .B O1 - O3 - O5 - - x x .TP .B E1 - - E4 - - - .TP .B - - - - - - - - - .TP .B - - .PP Newline separates arguments, but newline at the end of file is treated as an empty argument. So this runs 2 jobs: .TP .nf echo two_jobs | threader -run 'echo "$THREADID"' .fi .PP .B threader ignores stderr, so any output to stderr is lost. .B threader buffers in RAM, so output bigger than the machine's virtual memory will cause the machine to crash. .PP https://github.com/voodooEntity/threader (Last checked: 2024-06) .SS DIFFERENCES BETWEEN runp AND GNU Parallel .PP Summary (see legend above): .TP .B I1 I2 - - - - - .SS DIFFERENCES BETWEEN SHELL_EXECUTOR AND GNU Parallel .PP .B SHELL_EXECUTOR has a built-in way to handle the case where the number of arguments exceeds the command line length limit. GNU .B parallel does this by default. .PP .B SHELL_EXECUTOR also prints the job number before each line of output. GNU .B parallel can do this with the .B --tag option. .PP .B SHELL_EXECUTOR can also execute commands in parallel based on a list of arguments or files, similar to GNU .B parallel . .PP .B SHELL_EXECUTOR also has a built-in way to handle the case where the number of jobs exceeds the number of CPUs. GNU .B parallel does this by default. .PP .B SHELL_EXECUTOR also has a built-in way to handle the case where the number of jobs exceeds the available memory. GNU .B parallel can do this with the .B --memfree option. .PP The system may freeze if there are so many jobs that there is not enough memory to run them all at the same time. .PP https://github.com/royriojas/shell-executor (Last checked: 2024-06) .SH AUTHOR Written by Ole Tange. .SH REPORTING BUGS Report bugs to . .SH COPYRIGHT Copyright © 2024 Free Software Foundation, Inc. It takes 1s per 1 million output lines. .PP .B par needs to have all commands before starting the first job. The jobs are read from stdin (standard input) so any quoting will have to be done by the user. .PP Stdout (standard output) is prepended with o:. Stderr (standard error) is sendt to stdout (standard output) and prepended with e:. .PP For short jobs with little output .B par is 20% faster than GNU .B parallel and 60% slower than .B xargs . .PP https://github.com/UnixJunkie/PAR .PP https://savannah.nongnu.org/projects/par (Last checked: 2019-02) .SS DIFFERENCES BETWEEN fd AND GNU Parallel .PP .B fd does not support composed commands, so commands must be wrapped in .B sh -c . .PP It buffers output in RAM. .PP It only takes file names from the filesystem as input (similar to .B find ). .PP https://github.com/sharkdp/fd (Last checked: 2019-02) .SS DIFFERENCES BETWEEN lateral AND GNU Parallel .PP .B lateral is very similar to .B sem : It takes a single command and runs it in the background. Composed commands do not work. .PP .B with-this gives some additional information, so the output has to be cleaned before piping it to the next command. .PP https://github.com/amritb/with-this.git (Last checked: 2024-06) .SS DIFFERENCES BETWEEN Tollef's parallel (moreutils) AND GNU Parallel .PP Summary (see legend above): .TP .B - - - I4 - - I7 .TP .B - - M3 - - M6 .TP .B - O2 O3 - O5 O6 - x x .TP .B E1 - - - - - E7 .TP .B - x x x x x x x x .TP .B - - .PP .I EXAMPLES FROM Tollef's parallel MANUAL .PP .B Tollef parallel sh -c "echo hi; sleep 2; echo bye" -- 1 2 3 .PP .B GNU parallel "echo hi; sleep 2; echo bye" ::: 1 2 3 .PP .B Tollef parallel -j 3 ufraw -o processed -- *.NEF .PP .B GNU parallel -j 3 ufraw -o processed ::: *.NEF .PP .B Tollef parallel -j 3 -- ls df "echo hi" .PP .B GNU parallel -j 3 ::: ls df "echo hi" .PP (Last checked: 2019-08) .SS DIFFERENCES BETWEEN rargs AND GNU Parallel .PP Summary (see legend above): .TP .B I1 - - - - - I7 .TP .B - - M3 M4 - - .TP .B - O2 O3 - O5 O6 - O8 - .TP .B E1 - - E4 - - - .TP .B - - - - - - - - - .TP .B - - .PP .B rargs has elegant ways of doing named regexp capture and field ranges. .PP With GNU .B parallel you can use .B --rpl to get a similar functionality as regexp capture gives, and use .B join and .BR .I @arg .BR to get the field ranges. But the syntax is longer. This: .TP .nf --rpl '{r(\d+)..(\d+)} $_=join("..",@arg[$$1..$$2])' .fi .PP would make it possible to use: .TP .nf {1r3..6} .fi .PP for field 3..6. .PP For full support of {n..m:s} including negative numbers use a dynamic replacement string like this: .TP .nf PARALLEL=--rpl\ \''{r((-?\d+)?)..((-?\d+)?)((:([^}]*))?)} $a = defined $$2 ? $$2 < 0 ? 1+$#arg+$$2 : $$2 : 1; $b = defined $$4 ? $$4 < 0 ? 1+$#arg+$$4 : $$4 : $#arg+1; $s = defined $$6 ? $$7 : " "; $_ = join $s,@arg[$a..$b]'\' export PARALLEL .fi .PP You can then do: .TP .nf head /etc/passwd | parallel --colsep : echo ..={1r..} ..3={1r..3} \ 4..={1r4..} 2..4={1r2..4} 3..3={1r3..3} ..3:-={1r..3:-} \ ..3:/={1r..3:/} -1={-1} -5={-5} -6={-6} -3..={1r-3..} .fi .PP .I EXAMPLES FROM rargs MANUAL .PP .TP .nf 1$ ls *.bak | rargs -p '(.*)\.bak' mv {0} {1} 1$ ls *.bak | parallel mv {} {.} 2$ cat download-list.csv | rargs -p '(?P.*),(?P.*)' wget {url} -O {filename} 2$ cat download-list.csv | parallel --csv wget {1} -O {2} # or use regexps: 2$ cat download-list.csv | parallel --rpl '{url} s/,.*//' --rpl '{filename} s/.*?,//' \ wget {url} -O {filename} 3$ cat /etc/passwd | rargs -d: echo -e 'id: "{1}"\t name: "{5}"\t rest: "{6..::}"' 3$ cat /etc/passwd | parallel -q --colsep : \ echo -e 'id: "{1}"\t name: "{5}"\t rest: "{=6 $_=join(":",@arg[6..$#arg])=}"' .fi .PP https://github.com/lotabout/rargs (Last checked: 2020-01) .SS DIFFERENCES BETWEEN threader AND GNU Parallel .PP Summary (see legend above): .TP .B I1 - - - - - - .TP .B M1 - M3 - - M6 .TP .B O1 - O3 - O5 - - x x .TP .B E1 - - E4 - - - .TP .B - - - - - - - - - .TP .B - - .PP Newline separates arguments, but newline at the end of file is treated as an empty argument. So this runs 2 jobs: .TP .nf echo two_jobs | threader -run 'echo "$THREADID"' .fi .PP .B threader ignores stderr, so any output to stderr is lost. .B threader buffers in RAM, so output bigger than the machine's virtual memory will cause the machine to crash. .PP https://github.com/voodooEntity/threader (Last checked: 2024-06) .SS DIFFERENCES BETWEEN runp AND GNU Parallel .PP Summary (see legend above): .TP .B I1 I2 - - - - - .SS DIFFERENCES BETWEEN SHELL_EXECUTOR AND GNU Parallel .PP .B SHELL_EXECUTOR has a built-in way to handle the case where the number of arguments exceeds the command line length limit. GNU .B parallel does this by default. .PP .B SHELL_EXECUTOR also prints the job number before each line of output. GNU .B parallel can do this with the .B --tag option. .PP .B SHELL_EXECUTOR can also execute commands in parallel based on a list of arguments or files, similar to GNU .B parallel . .PP .B SHELL_EXECUTOR also has a built-in way to handle the case where the number of jobs exceeds the number of CPUs. GNU .B parallel does this by default. .PP .B SHELL_EXECUTOR also has a built-in way to handle the case where the number of jobs exceeds the available memory. GNU .B parallel can do this with the .B --memfree option. .PP The system may freeze if there are so many jobs that there is not enough memory to run them all at the same time. .PP https://github.com/royriojas/shell-executor (Last checked: 2024-06) .SH AUTHOR Written by Ole Tange. .SH REPORTING BUGS Report bugs to . .SH COPYRIGHT Copyright © 2024 Free Software Foundation, Inc.
