M1 - (M3) - - M6
O1 O2 O3 - O5 O6 - x x -
E1 - - - - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

(M3): Puedes agregar un prefijo y un sufijo a la entrada, por lo que significa que solo puedes insertar el argumento en la línea de comandos una vez.

runp ejecuta 10 trabajos en paralelo por defecto. runp se bloquea si la salida de un comando es > 64 Kbytes. Se necesita entrecomillar la entrada. Agrega salida a stderr (esto se puede evitar con -q)

Ejemplos como GNU Parallel

base='https://images-api.nasa.gov/search'
query='jupiter'
desc='planet'
type='image'
url="$base?q=$query&description=$desc&media_type=$type"

# Descarga las imágenes en paralelo usando runp
curl -s $url | jq -r .collection.items[].href | \
  runp -p 'curl -s' | jq -r .[] | grep large | \
  runp -p 'curl -s -L -O'
time curl -s $url | jq -r .collection.items[].href | \
  runp -g 1 -q -p 'curl -s' | jq -r .[] | grep large | \
  runp -g 1 -q -p 'curl -s -L -O'
# Descarga las imágenes en paralelo
curl -s $url | jq -r .collection.items[].href | \
  parallel curl -s | jq -r .[] | grep large | \
  parallel curl -s -L -O

time curl -s $url | jq -r .collection.items[].href | \
  parallel -j 1 curl -s | jq -r .[] | grep large | \
  parallel -j 1 curl -s -L -O

Ejecuta algunos comandos de prueba (leer desde archivo)

# Crea un archivo que contenga comandos para ejecutar en paralelo.
cat << EOF > /tmp/test-commands.txt
sleep 5
sleep 3
blah     # esto fallará
ls $PWD  # la variable de entorno PWD se usa aquí
EOF

# Ejecuta comandos desde el archivo.
runp /tmp/test-commands.txt > /dev/null

parallel -a /tmp/test-commands.txt > /dev/null

Haz ping a varios hosts y observa la pérdida de paquetes (leer desde stdin)

# Primero copia esta línea y presiona Enter
runp -p 'ping -c 5 -W 2' -s '| grep loss'
localhost
1.1.1.1
8.8.8.8
# Presiona Enter y Ctrl-D cuando hayas terminado de ingresar los hosts
# Primero copia esta línea y presiona Enter
parallel ping -c 5 -W 2 {} '| grep loss'
localhost
1.1.1.1
8.8.8.8
# Presiona Enter y Ctrl-D cuando hayas terminado de ingresar los hosts

Obtiene los tamaños de los directorios (leer desde stdin)

echo -e "$HOME\n/etc\n/tmp" | runp -q -p 'sudo du -sh'
echo -e "$HOME\n/etc\n/tmp" | parallel sudo du -sh
# o:
parallel sudo du -sh ::: "$HOME" /etc /tmp

Comprime archivos

find . -iname '*.txt' | runp -p 'gzip --best'
find . -iname '*.txt' | parallel gzip --best

Mide el tiempo de solicitud + respuesta HTTP

export CURL="curl -w 'time_total:  %{time_total}\n'"
CURL="$CURL -o /dev/null -s https://golang.org/"
perl -wE 'for (1..10) { say $ENV{CURL} }' |
   runp -q  # Realiza 10 solicitudes
perl -wE 'for (1..10) { say $ENV{CURL} }' | parallel
# o:
parallel -N0 "$CURL" ::: {1..10}

Encuentra puertos TCP abiertos

cat << EOF > /tmp/host-port.txt
localhost 22
localhost 80
localhost 81
127.0.0.1 443
127.0.0.1 444
scanme.nmap.org 22
scanme.nmap.org 23
scanme.nmap.org 443
EOF

1$ cat /tmp/host-port.txt |
     runp -q -p 'netcat -v -w2 -z' 2>&1 | egrep '(succeeded!|open)$'

# --colsep es necesario para dividir la línea
1$ cat /tmp/host-port.txt |
     parallel --colsep ' ' netcat -v -w2 -z 2>&1 |
     egrep '(succeeded!|open)$'
# o usa uq para sin comillas:
1$ cat /tmp/host-port.txt |
     parallel netcat -v -w2 -z {=uq=} 2>&1 |
     egrep '(succeeded!|open)$'

https://github.com/jreisinger/runp (Última verificación: 2020-04)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE papply Y GNU Parallel

Resumen (ver leyenda arriba):

- - - I4 - - -
M1 - M3 - - M6
- - O3 - O5 - - x x O10
E1 - - E4 - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

papply no imprime la salida si el comando falla:

$ papply 'echo %F; false' foo
"echo foo; false" no tuvo éxito

Las cadenas de reemplazo de papply (%F %d %f %n %e %z) se pueden simular en GNU parallel poniendo esto en ~/.parallel/config:

--rpl '%F'
--rpl '%d $_=Q(::dirname($_));'
--rpl '%f s:.*/::;'
--rpl '%n s:.*/::;s:\.[^/.]+$::;'
--rpl '%e s:.*\.:.:'
--rpl '%z $_=""'

papply almacena en búfer en RAM y usa el doble de la cantidad de salida. Por lo tanto, una salida de 5 GB requiere 10 GB de RAM.

El almacenamiento en búfer consume mucha CPU: Almacenar en búfer una línea de 5 GB toma 40 segundos (en comparación con 10 segundos con GNU parallel).

Ejemplos como GNU Parallel

1$ papply gzip *.txt

1$ parallel gzip ::: *.txt

2$ papply "convert %F %n.jpg" *.png

2$ parallel convert {} {.}.jpg ::: *.png

https://pypi.org/project/papply/ (Última verificación: 2020-04)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE async Y GNU Parallel

Resumen (ver leyenda arriba):

- - - I4 - - I7
- - - - - M6
- O2 O3 - O5 O6 - x x O10
E1 - - E4 - E6 -
- - - - - - - - -
S1 S2

async es muy similar al modo --semaphore de GNU parallel (también conocido como sem). async requiere que el usuario inicie un proceso servidor.

La entrada está entrecomillada como -q, por lo que necesita bash -c "...;..." para ejecutar comandos compuestos.

Ejemplos como GNU Parallel

1$ S="/tmp/example_socket"

1$ ID=myid

2$ async -s="$S" server --start

2$ # GNU Parallel no necesita un servidor para ejecutarse

3$ for i in {1..20}; do
       # imprime la salida del comando en stdout
       async -s="$S" cmd -- bash -c "sleep 1 && echo test $i"
   done

3$ for i in {1..20}; do
       # imprime la salida del comando en stdout
       sem --id "$ID" -j100% "sleep 1 && echo test $i"
       # GNU Parallel solo imprimirá el trabajo cuando esté terminado
       # Si necesitas que la salida de diferentes trabajos se mezcle
       # usa -u o --line-buffer
       sem --id "$ID" -j100% --line-buffer "sleep 1 && echo test $i"
   done

4$ # espera hasta que todos los comandos hayan terminado
   async -s="$S" wait

4$ sem --id "$ID" --wait

5$ # configura el servidor para ejecutar cuatro comandos en paralelo
   async -s="$S" server -j4

5$ export PARALLEL=-j4

6$ mkdir "/tmp/ex_dir"
   for i in {21..40}; do
     # redirige la salida del comando a /tmp/ex_dir/file*
     async -s="$S" cmd -o "/tmp/ex_dir/file$i" -- \
       bash -c "sleep 1 && echo test $i"
   done

6$ mkdir "/tmp/ex_dir"
   for i in {21..40}; do
     # redirige la salida del comando a /tmp/ex_dir/file*
     sem --id "$ID" --result '/tmp/my-ex/file-{=$_=""=}'"$i" \
       "sleep 1 && echo test $i"
   done

7$ sem --id "$ID" --wait

7$ async -s="$S" wait

8$ # detiene el servidor
   async -s="$S" server --stop

8$ # GNU Parallel no necesita detener un servidor

https://github.com/ctbur/async/ (Última verificación: 2024-06)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE pardi Y GNU Parallel

Resumen (ver leyenda arriba):

I1 I2 - - - - I7
M1 - - - - M6
O1 O2 O3 O4 O5 - O7 - - O10
E1 - - E4 - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

pardi es muy similar a parallel --pipe --cat: Lee bloques de datos y no argumentos. Por lo tanto, no puede insertar un argumento en la línea de comandos. Coloca el bloque en un archivo temporal, y este nombre de archivo (%IN) puede colocarse en la línea de comandos. Solo puedes usar %IN una vez.

También puede ejecutar líneas de comandos completas en paralelo (como: cat file | parallel).

EJEMPLOS DE pardi test.sh

1$ time pardi -v -c 100 -i data/decoys.smi -ie .smi -oe .smi \
     -o data/decoys_std_pardi.smi \
        -w '(standardiser -i %IN -o %OUT 2>&1) > /dev/null'

1$ cat data/decoys.smi |
     time parallel -N 100 --pipe --cat \
       '(standardiser -i {} -o {#} 2>&1) > /dev/null; cat {#}; rm {#}' \
       > data/decoys_std_pardi.smi

2$ pardi -n 1 -i data/test_in.types -o data/test_out.types \
           -d 'r:^#atoms:' -w 'cat %IN > %OUT'

2$ cat data/test_in.types |
     parallel -n 1 -k --pipe --cat --regexp --recstart '^#atoms' \
       'cat {}' > data/test_out.types

3$ pardi -c 6 -i data/test_in.types -o data/test_out.types \
           -d 'r:^#atoms:' -w 'cat %IN > %OUT'

3$ cat data/test_in.types |
     parallel -n 6 -k --pipe --cat --regexp --recstart '^#atoms' \
       'cat {}' > data/test_out.types

4$ pardi -i data/decoys.mol2 -o data/still_decoys.mol2 \
           -d 's:@<TRIPOS>MOLECULE' -w 'cp %IN %OUT'

4$ cat data/decoys.mol2 |
     parallel -n 1 --pipe --cat --recstart '@<TRIPOS>MOLECULE' \
       'cp {} {#}; cat {#}; rm {#}' > data/still_decoys.mol2

5$ pardi -i data/decoys.mol2 -o data/decoys2.mol2 \
           -d b:10000 -w 'cp %IN %OUT' --preserve

5$ cat data/decoys.mol2 |
     parallel -k --pipe --block 10k --recend '' --cat \
       'cat {} > {#}; cat {#}; rm {#}' > data/decoys2.mol2

https://github.com/UnixJunkie/pardi (Última verificación: 2021-01)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE bthread Y GNU Parallel

Resumen (ver leyenda arriba):

- - - I4 - - -
- - - - - M6
O1 - O3 - - - O7 O8 - -
E1 - - - - - -
- - - -
- - - - -
- -

bthread tarda aproximadamente 1 segundo por MB de salida. La longitud máxima de línea de salida es 1073741759.

No se puede citar espacios en el comando, por lo que no se pueden ejecutar comandos compuestos como sh -c "echo a; echo b".

https://gitlab.com/netikras/bthread (Última revisión: 2024-06)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE simple_gpu_scheduler Y GNU Parallel

Resumen (ver leyenda arriba):

I1 - - - - - I7
M1 - - - - M6
- O2 O3 - - O6 - x x O10
E1 - - - - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

EJEMPLOS DEL MANUAL DE simple_gpu_scheduler

1$ simple_gpu_scheduler --gpus 0 1 2 < gpu_commands.txt
1$ parallel -j3 --shuf \
   CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES='{=1 $_=slot()-1 =} {=uq;=}' \
     < gpu_commands.txt
2$ simple_hypersearch \
     "python3 train_dnn.py --lr {lr} --batch_size {bs}" \
     -p lr 0.001 0.0005 0.0001 -p bs 32 64 128 |
     simple_gpu_scheduler --gpus 0,1,2
2$ parallel --header : --shuf -j3 -v \
     CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES='{=1 $_=slot()-1 =}' \
     python3 train_dnn.py --lr {lr} --batch_size {bs} \
     ::: lr 0.001 0.0005 0.0001 ::: bs 32 64 128
3$ simple_hypersearch \
     "python3 train_dnn.py --lr {lr} --batch_size {bs}" \
     --n-samples 5 -p lr 0.001 0.0005 0.0001 -p bs 32 64 128 |
     simple_gpu_scheduler --gpus 0,1,2
3$ parallel --header : --shuf \
     CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES='{=1 $_=slot()-1; seq()>5 and skip() =}' \
     python3 train_dnn.py --lr {lr} --batch_size {bs} \
     ::: lr 0.001 0.0005 0.0001 ::: bs 32 64 128
4$ touch gpu.queue
   tail -f -n 0 gpu.queue | simple_gpu_scheduler --gpus 0,1,2 &
   echo "my_command_with | and stuff > logfile" >> gpu.queue
4$ touch gpu.queue
   tail -f -n 0 gpu.queue |
     parallel -j3 CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES='{=1 $_=slot()-1 =} {=uq;=}' &
   # Necesario para llenar las ranuras de trabajo una vez
   seq 3 | parallel echo true >> gpu.queue
   # Añadir trabajos
   echo "my_command_with | and stuff > logfile" >> gpu.queue
   # Necesario para vaciar la salida de los trabajos completados
   seq 3 | parallel echo true >> gpu.queue

https://github.com/ExpectationMax/simple_gpu_scheduler (Última revisión: 2024-06)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE parasweep Y GNU Parallel

parasweep es un módulo de Python para facilitar la ejecución paralela de barridos de parámetros.

Un trabajo de parasweep normalmente tomará un archivo de texto como entrada. El archivo de texto contiene los argumentos para el trabajo. Algunos de estos argumentos serán fijos y otros serán modificados por parasweep.

Lo hace teniendo un archivo de plantilla como template.txt:

Xval: {x}
Yval: {y}
FixedValue: 9
# x con 2 decimales
DecimalX: {x:.2f}
TenX: ${x*10}
RandomVal: {r}

y a partir de esta plantilla genera el archivo a ser utilizado por el trabajo reemplazando las cadenas de reemplazo.

Al ser un módulo de Python, parasweep se integra más estrechamente con Python que GNU parallel. Obtienes los parámetros directamente en una estructura de datos de Python. Con GNU parallel, puedes usar el formato de salida JSON o CSV para obtener algo similar, pero tendrías que leer la salida.

parasweep tiene un método de filtrado para ignorar combinaciones de parámetros que no necesitas.

En lugar de llamar directamente a los trabajos, parasweep puede usar la API de Python para la Gestión de Recursos Distribuidos (Distributed Resource Management Application API) para hacer que los trabajos se ejecuten con diferente software de clúster.

GNU parallel --tmpl soporta plantillas con cadenas de reemplazo. Como por ejemplo:

Xval: {x}
Yval: {y}
FixedValue: 9
# x con 2 decimales
DecimalX: {=x $_=sprintf("%.2f",$_) =}
TenX: {=x $_=$_*10 =}
RandomVal: {=1 $_=rand() =}

que se puede usar así:

parallel --header : --tmpl my.tmpl={#}.t myprog {#}.t \
  ::: x 1 2 3 ::: y 1 2 3

El filtrado se soporta así:

parallel --filter '{1} > {2}' echo ::: 1 2 3 ::: 1 2 3

https://github.com/eviatarbach/parasweep (Última revisión: 2021-01)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE parallel-bash(2021) Y GNU Parallel

Resumen (ver leyenda arriba):

I1 I2 - - - - -
- - M3 - - M6
- O2 O3 - O5 O6 - O8 x O10
E1 - - - - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

parallel-bash está escrito en bash puro. Es realmente rápido (overhead de ~0.05 ms/trabajo en comparación con los 3-10 ms/trabajo de GNU parallel). Así que si tus trabajos tienen una duración extremadamente corta, y puedes vivir con el comando bastante limitado, esto puede ser útil.

Funciona creando una cola para cada proceso. Luego, los trabajos se distribuyen a las colas en orden aleatorio. Finalmente, las colas se inician en paralelo. Esto funciona bien, si tienes suerte, pero si no, todos los trabajos largos pueden terminar en la misma cola, por lo que puedes ver:

$ printf "%b\n" 1 1 1 4 1 1 1 4 1 1 1 4 |
    time parallel -P4 sleep {}
(7 segundos)
$ printf "%b\n" 1 1 1 4 1 1 1 4 1 1 1 4 |
    time parallel-bash -p 4 -c sleep {}
(12 segundos)

Debido a que utiliza listas de bash, el número total de trabajos está limitado a 167000..265000 dependiendo de tu entorno. Obtienes un error de segmentación cuando alcanzas el límite.

Ctrl-C no detiene la creación de nuevos trabajos. Ctrl-Z no suspende los trabajos en ejecución.

EJEMPLOS DE parallel-bash

1$ some_input | parallel-bash -p 5 -c echo
1$ some_input | parallel -j 5 echo
2$ parallel-bash -p 5 -c echo < some_file
2$ parallel -j 5 echo < some_file
3$ parallel-bash -p 5 -c echo <<< 'some string'
3$ parallel -j 5 -c echo <<< 'some string'
4$ something | parallel-bash -p 5 -c echo {} {}
4$ something | parallel -j 5 echo {} {}

https://github.com/Akianonymus/parallel-bash/ (Última revisión: 2024-06)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE parallel-bash(2024) Y GNU Parallel

Resumen (ver leyenda arriba):

I1 I2 - - - - -
- - M3 - - M6
- O2 O3 - O5 O6 - O8 x O10
E1 - - - - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

parallel-bash está escrito en bash puro. Es realmente rápido (overhead de ~0.05 ms/trabajo en comparación con los 3-10 ms/trabajo de GNU parallel). Así que si tus trabajos tienen una duración extremadamente corta, y puedes vivir con el comando bastante limitado, esto puede ser útil.

Parece que el número de trabajos debe ser divisible por -p, por lo que a veces no ejecuta los trabajos:

# No hace nada
$ seq 3 | parallel-bash -p 4 bash -c 'touch myfile-{}'

Esto debería crear myfile-1..3, pero no crea nada.

Divide la entrada en colas. Cada cola tiene una longitud de -p. Por lo tanto, esto creará 250 colas y ejecutará los 250 procesos en paralelo:

$ seq 1000 | parallel-bash -p 4 bash -c 'sleep {}'

Esto es muy diferente de parallel-bash(2021) donde -p es el número de trabajadores, similar a --jobs en GNU parallel.

En otras palabras: parallel-bash *no* garantiza que solo se ejecutarán 4 trabajos en paralelo. Esto puede sobrecargar tu máquina:

# Advertencia: Esto iniciará 25000 procesos, no solo 4
$ seq 100000 | parallel-bash -p 4 sleep {}

Si tienes mala suerte, todos los trabajos largos pueden terminar en la misma cola:

$ printf "%b\n" 1 1 1 1 5 5 5 5 1 1 1 1 |
    time parallel -P4 sleep {}
(7 segundos)
$ printf "%b\n" 1 1 1 1 5 5 5 5 1 1 1 1 |
    time ./parallel-bash.bash -p 4 -c sleep {}
(20 segundos)

Ctrl-C mata los trabajos (como se esperaba). Ctrl-Z no suspende los trabajos en ejecución.

EJEMPLOS DE parallel-bash

1$ main() { echo "${1}" ;}
   export -f main
1$ printf "%b\n" {1..1000} | ./parallel-bash -p 10 main {}
1$ printf "%b\n" {1..1000} | parallel -j 100 main {}
2$ # El número de entradas debe ser divisible por 5
   some_input | parallel-bash -p 5 echo
2$ some_input | parallel -j 5 echo
3$ # El número de entradas debe ser divisible por 5
   parallel-bash -p 5 echo < some_file
3$ parallel -j 5 echo < some_file
4$ # El número de líneas en 'some string' debe ser divisible por 5
   parallel-bash -p 5 echo <<< 'some string'
4$ parallel -j 5 -c echo <<< 'some string'
5$ something | parallel-bash -p 5 echo {}
5$ something | parallel -j 5 echo {}

https://github.com/Akianonymus/parallel-bash/ (Última revisión: 2024-06)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE bash-concurrent Y GNU Parallel

bash-concurrent es más una alternativa a make que a GNU parallel. Su entrada es muy similar a un Makefile, donde los trabajos dependen de otros trabajos.

Tiene un bonito indicador de progreso donde puedes ver qué trabajos se completaron con éxito, qué trabajos se están ejecutando actualmente, qué trabajos fallaron y qué trabajos se omitieron debido a que un trabajo dependiente falló. El indicador no se adapta bien al redimensionamiento de la ventana.

La salida se almacena en caché en archivos temporales en disco, pero solo se muestra si hay un error, por lo que no está pensada para formar parte de una tubería de UNIX. Si bash-concurrent falla, estos archivos temporales no se eliminan.

Utiliza un algoritmo O(n*n), por lo que si tienes 1000 trabajos independientes, tarda 22 segundos en iniciarse.

https://github.com/themattrix/bash-concurrent (Última revisión: 2024-06)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE spawntool Y GNU Parallel

Resumen (ver leyenda arriba):

I1 - - - - - -

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN xargs AND GNU Parallel

Summary (see legend above):

I1 - - - - - -
M1 - M3 - - M6
O1 - O3 O4 - - - x x -
E1 - - - - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

GNU Parallel is a general parallelizer and a drop-in replacement for xargs.

This means that all invocations of xargs can be replaced by GNU Parallel.

find . -name '*.jpg' | xargs -n 1 libexif-tools/exiftool
find . -name '*.jpg' | parallel -n 1 libexif-tools/exiftool

GNU Parallel is often faster than xargs.

seq 10000 | xargs sleep
seq 10000 | parallel --n 1 sleep

GNU Parallel can also run jobs in background and close them explicitly:

seq 10000 | parallel --tag --joblog joblog.txt sleep

GNU Parallel can also start jobs by arguments instead of piping:

parallel --tag --joblog joblog.txt sleep ::: $(seq 10000)

GNU Parallel can also start jobs on remote SSH hosts:

parallel --tag --onall 'ssh user@host "hostname"'

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN GNU Parallel AND parallel

Summary (see legend above):

- - - - - M6
- O2 O3 - O5 O6 - x x -
E1 - - - - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

The parallel utility from GNU Parallel is the original tool. There is a new version of GNU Parallel (see GNU Parallel above) that has a lot more functionality.

1$ seq 5 | parallel echo {}
1$ parallel echo ::: $(seq 5)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN loom AND GNU Parallel

Summary (see legend above):

- - - - - M6
- O2 O3 - O5 O6 - x x -
E1 - - - - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

loom is a shell script that is used to run jobs in parallel.

It is not as flexible as GNU Parallel.

1$ loom -c 4 -m 1000 echo {1..5}
1$ parallel -j 4 --memfree 1000M echo {1..5}

https://github.com/joel/loom (Last checked: 2024-05)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN go-pssh AND GNU Parallel

Summary (see legend above):

- - - - -
O1 - - - - - - x x O10
E1 - - - - - -
R1 R2 - - - R6 - - -
- -

go-pssh does ssh in parallel to multiple machines. It runs the same command on multiple machines similar to --nonall.

The hostnames must be given as IP-addresses (not as hostnames).

Output is sent to stdout (standard output) if command is successful, and to stderr (standard error) if the command fails.

EXAMPLES FROM go-pssh

1$ go-pssh -l <ip>,<ip> -u <user> -p <port> -P <passwd> -c "<command>"
1$ parallel -S 'sshpass -p <passwd> ssh -p <port> <user>@<ip>' \
     --nonall "<command>"
2$ go-pssh scp -f host.txt -u <user> -p <port> -P <password> \
     -s /local/file_or_directory -d /remote/directory
2$ parallel --nonall --slf host.txt \
     --basefile /local/file_or_directory/./ --wd /remote/directory
     --ssh 'sshpass -p <password> ssh -p <port> -l <user>' true
3$ go-pssh scp -l <ip>,<ip> -u <user> -p <port> -P <password> \
     -s /local/file_or_directory -d /remote/directory
3$ parallel --nonall -S <ip>,<ip> \
     --basefile /local/file_or_directory/./ --wd /remote/directory
     --ssh 'sshpass -p <password> ssh -p <port> -l <user>' true

https://github.com/xuchenCN/go-pssh (Last checked: 2021-07)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN go-parallel AND GNU Parallel

Summary (see legend above):

I1 I2 - - - - (I7)
- - M3 - - M6
- O2 O3 - O5 - - x x -
E1 - - E4 - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

go-parallel uses Go templates for replacement strings. Quite similar to the {= perl expr =} replacement string.

The basic replacement strings can be emulated by putting this into ~/.parallel/config:

--rpl '{{.Input}} '
--rpl '{{.Time}} use DateTime; $_= DateTime->from_epoch(time);'
--rpl '{{.Start}} use DateTime; $_= DateTime->from_epoch($^T);'

Then you can do:

seq 10 | parallel sleep {{.Input}}';' echo {{.Start}} {{.Time}}
seq 10 | go-parallel -t 'bash -c "sleep {{.Input}}; echo \"{{.Start}}\" \"{{.Time}}\""'

If the input is too long (64K), you get no error:

perl -e 'print "works."."x"x100' | parallel.go -t 'echo {{noExt .Input}} '
perl -e 'print "fails."."x"x100_000_000' | parallel.go -t 'echo {{noExt .Input}} '

Special chars are quoted:

echo '"&+<>' | go-parallel echo
"&+<>

but not shell quoted when using replacement strings:

echo '"&+<>' | go-parallel -t 'echo {{.Input}}'
&#34;&amp;&#43;&lt;&gt;

EXAMPLES FROM go-parallel

1$ go-parallel -a ./files.txt -t 'cp {{.Input}} {{.Input | dirname | dirname}}'
1$ parallel -a ./files.txt cp {} '{= $_=::dirname(::dirname($_)) =}'
2$ go-parallel -a ./files.txt -t 'mkdir -p {{.Input}} {{noExt .Input}}'
2$ parallel -a ./files.txt echo mkdir -p {} {.}
3$ go-parallel -a ./files.txt -t 'mkdir -p {{.Input}} {{.Input | basename | noExt}}'
3$ parallel -a ./files.txt echo mkdir -p {} {/.}
4$ time find ~/src/go -type f | go-parallel md5sum > /dev/null
4$ time find ~/src/go -type f | parallel md5sum > /dev/null
   # Though you would probably do this instead:
   time find ~/src/go -type f | parallel -X md5sum > /dev/null

https://github.com/mylanconnolly/parallel (Last checked: 2024-06)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN p AND GNU Parallel

Summary (see legend above):

- - - I4 - - x
- - - - - M6
- O2 O3 - O5 O6 - x x -
E1 - - - - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

p is a tiny shell script. It can color output with some predefined colors, but is otherwise quite limited.

It maxes out at around 116000 jobs (probably due to limitations in Bash).

EXAMPLES FROM p

Some of the examples from p cannot be implemented 100% by GNU parallel: The coloring is a bit different, and GNU parallel cannot have --tag for some inputs and not for others.

The coloring done by GNU parallel is not exactly the same as p.

1$ p -bc blue "ping 127.0.0.1" -uc red "ping 192.168.0.1" \
   -rc yellow "ping 192.168.1.1" -t example "ping example.com"
1$ parallel --lb -j0 --color --tag ping \
   ::: 127.0.0.1 192.168.0.1 192.168.1.1 example.com
2$ p "tail -f /var/log/httpd/access_log" \
   -bc red "tail -f /var/log/httpd/error_log"
2$ cd /var/log/httpd;
   parallel --lb --color --tag tail -f ::: access_log error_log
3$ p tail -f "some file" \& p tail -f "other file with space.txt"
3$ parallel --lb tail -f ::: 'some file' "other file with space.txt"
4$ p -t project1 "hg pull project1" -t project2 \
   "hg pull project2" -t project3 "hg pull project3"
4$ parallel --lb hg pull ::: project{1..3}

https://github.com/rudymatela/evenmoreutils/blob/master/man/p.1.adoc (Last checked: 2022-04)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN senechal AND GNU Parallel

Summary (see legend above):

I1 - - - - - -
M1 - M3 - - M6
O1 - O3 O4 - - - x x -
E1 - - - - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

seneschal only starts the first job after reading the last job, and output from the first job is only printed after the last job finishes.

1 byte of output requites 3.5 bytes of RAM.

This makes it impossible to have a total output bigger than the virtual memory.

Even though output is kept in RAM outputing is quite slow: 30 MB/s.

Output larger than 4 GB causes random problems - it looks like a race condition.

This:

echo 1 | seneschal  --prefix='yes `seq 1000`|head -c 1G' >/dev/null

takes 4100(!) CPU seconds to run on a 64C64T server, but only 140 CPU seconds on a 4C8T laptop. So it looks like seneschal wastes a lot of CPU time coordinating the CPUs.

Compare this to:

echo 1 | time -v parallel -N0 'yes `seq 1000`|head -c 1G' >/dev/null

which takes 3-8 CPU seconds.

EXAMPLES FROM seneschal README.md

1$ echo $REPOS | seneschal --prefix="cd {} && git pull"
# If $REPOS is newline separated
1$ echo "$REPOS" | parallel -k "cd {} && git pull"
# If $REPOS is space separated
1$ echo -n "$REPOS" | parallel -d' ' -k "cd {} && git pull"
COMMANDS="pwd
sleep 5 && echo boom
echo Howdy
whoami"

2$ echo "$COMMANDS" | seneschal --debug

2$ echo "$COMMANDS" | parallel -k -v

3$ ls -1 | seneschal --prefix="pushd {}; git pull; popd;"

3$ ls -1 | parallel -k "pushd {}; git pull; popd;"
# Or if current dir also contains files:
3$ parallel -k "pushd {}; git pull; popd;" ::: */

https://github.com/TheWizardTower/seneschal (Last checked: 2022-06)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN async AND GNU Parallel

Summary (see legend above):

x x x x x x x
- x x x x x
x O2 O3 O4 O5 O6 - x x O10
E1 - - E4 - - -
- - - - - - - - -
S1 S2

async works like sem.

EXAMPLES FROM async

1$ S="/tmp/example_socket"
   async -s="$S" server --start
   for i in {1..20}; do
       # prints command output to stdout
       async -s="$S" cmd -- bash -c "sleep 1 && echo test $i"
   done

   # wait until all commands are finished
   async -s="$S" wait

1$ S="example_id"
   # server not needed
   for i in {1..20}; do
       # prints command output to stdout
       sem --bg --id "$S" -j100% "sleep 1 && echo test $i"
   done

   # wait until all commands are finished
   sem --fg --id "$S" --wait

2$ # configure the server to run four commands in parallel
   async -s="$S" server -j4

   mkdir "/tmp/ex_dir"
   for i in {21..40}; do
       # redirects command output to /tmp/ex_dir/file*
       async -s="$S" cmd -o "/tmp/ex_dir/file$i" -- \
         bash -c "sleep 1 && echo test $i"

https://github.com/MikaelE86/async (Last checked: 2024-06)

loom is a shell script that is used to run jobs in parallel.

It is not as flexible as GNU Parallel.

1$ loom -c 4 -m 1000 echo {1..5}
1$ parallel -j 4 --memfree 1000M echo {1..5}

https://github.com/joel/loom (Last checked: 2024-05)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN doit AND GNU Parallel

- O2 O3 - O5 - - x x -

- - - - - - - - -
- -

doit is a task-dependent shell tool. It builds and runs tasks.

The tasks are defined in a dodo file.

1$ doit run
1$ parallel ::: seq 100

http://pydoit.org/ (Last checked: 2021-07)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN go-pssh AND GNU Parallel

Summary (see legend above):

- - - - -
O1 - - - - - - x x O10
E1 - - - - - -
R1 R2 - - - R6 - - -
- -

go-pssh does ssh in parallel to multiple machines. It runs the same command on multiple machines similar to --nonall.

The hostnames must be given as IP-addresses (not as hostnames).

Output is sent to stdout (standard output) if command is successful, and to stderr (standard error) if the command fails.

EXAMPLES FROM go-pssh

1$ go-pssh -l <ip>,<ip> -u <user> -p <port> -P <passwd> -c "<command>"
1$ parallel -S 'sshpass -p <passwd> ssh -p <port> <user>@<ip>' \
     --nonall "<command>"
2$ go-pssh scp -f host.txt -u <user> -p <port> -P <password> \
     -s /local/file_or_directory -d /remote/directory
2$ parallel --nonall --slf host.txt \
     --basefile /local/file_or_directory/./ --wd /remote/directory
     --ssh 'sshpass -p <password> ssh -p <port> -l <user>' true
3$ go-pssh scp -l <ip>,<ip> -u <user> -p <port> -P <password> \
     -s /local/file_or_directory -d /remote/directory
3$ parallel --nonall -S <ip>,<ip> \
     --basefile /local/file_or_directory/./ --wd /remote/directory
     --ssh 'sshpass -p <password> ssh -p <port> -l <user>' true

https://github.com/xuchenCN/go-pssh (Last checked: 2021-07)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN go-parallel AND GNU Parallel

Summary (see legend above):

I1 I2 - - - - (I7)
- - M3 - - M6
- O2 O3 - O5 - - x x -
E1 - - E4 - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

go-parallel uses Go templates for replacement strings. Quite similar to the {= perl expr =} replacement string.

The basic replacement strings can be emulated by putting this into ~/.parallel/config:

--rpl '{{.Input}} '
--rpl '{{.Time}} use DateTime; $_= DateTime->from_epoch(time);'
--rpl '{{.Start}} use DateTime; $_= DateTime->from_epoch($^T);'

Then you can do:

seq 10 | parallel sleep {{.Input}}';' echo {{.Start}} {{.Time}}
seq 10 | go-parallel -t 'bash -c "sleep {{.Input}}; echo \"{{.Start}}\" \"{{.Time}}\""'

If the input is too long (64K), you get no error:

perl -e 'print "works."."x"x100' | parallel.go -t 'echo {{noExt .Input}} '
perl -e 'print "fails."."x"x100_000_000' | parallel.go -t 'echo {{noExt .Input}} '

Special chars are quoted:

echo '"&+<>' | go-parallel echo
"&+<>

but not shell quoted when using replacement strings:

echo '"&+<>' | go-parallel -t 'echo {{.Input}}'
&#34;&amp;&#43;&lt;&gt;

EXAMPLES FROM go-parallel

1$ go-parallel -a ./files.txt -t 'cp {{.Input}} {{.Input | dirname | dirname}}'
1$ parallel -a ./files.txt cp {} '{= $_=::dirname(::dirname($_)) =}'
2$ go-parallel -a ./files.txt -t 'mkdir -p {{.Input}} {{noExt .Input}}'
2$ parallel -a ./files.txt echo mkdir -p {} {.}
3$ go-parallel -a ./files.txt -t 'mkdir -p {{.Input}} {{.Input | basename | noExt}}'
3$ parallel -a ./files.txt echo mkdir -p {} {/.}
4$ time find ~/src/go -type f | go-parallel md5sum > /dev/null
4$ time find ~/src/go -type f | parallel md5sum > /dev/null
   # Though you would probably do this instead:
   time find ~/src/go -type f | parallel -X md5sum > /dev/null

https://github.com/mylanconnolly/parallel (Last checked: 2024-06)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN p AND GNU Parallel

Summary (see legend above):

- - - I4 - - x
- - - - - M6
- O2 O3 - O5 O6 - x x -
E1 - - - - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

p is a tiny shell script. It can color output with some predefined colors, but is otherwise quite limited.

It maxes out at around 116000 jobs (probably due to limitations in Bash).

EXAMPLES FROM p

Some of the examples from p cannot be implemented 100% by GNU parallel: The coloring is a bit different, and GNU parallel cannot have --tag for some inputs and not for others.

The coloring done by GNU parallel is not exactly the same as p.

1$ p -bc blue "ping 127.0.0.1" -uc red "ping 192.168.0.1" \
   -rc yellow "ping 192.168.1.1" -t example "ping example.com"
1$ parallel --lb -j0 --color --tag ping \
   ::: 127.0.0.1 192.168.0.1 192.168.1.1 example.com
2$ p "tail -f /var/log/httpd/access_log" \
   -bc red "tail -f /var/log/httpd/error_log"
2$ cd /var/log/httpd;
   parallel --lb --color --tag tail -f ::: access_log error_log
3$ p tail -f "some file" \& p tail -f "other file with space.txt"
3$ parallel --lb tail -f ::: 'some file' "other file with space.txt"
4$ p -t project1 "hg pull project1" -t project2 \
   "hg pull project2" -t project3 "hg pull project3"
4$ parallel --lb hg pull ::: project{1..3}

https://github.com/rudymatela/evenmoreutils/blob/master/man/p.1.adoc (Last checked: 2022-04)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN senechal AND GNU Parallel

Summary (see legend above):

I1 - - - - - -
M1 - M3 - - M6
O1 - O3 O4 - - - x x -
E1 - - - - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

seneschal only starts the first job after reading the last job, and output from the first job is only printed after the last job finishes.

1 byte of output requites 3.5 bytes of RAM.

This makes it impossible to have a total output bigger than the virtual memory.

Even though output is kept in RAM outputing is quite slow: 30 MB/s.

Output larger than 4 GB causes random problems - it looks like a race condition.

This:

echo 1 | seneschal  --prefix='yes `seq 1000`|head -c 1G' >/dev/null

takes 4100(!) CPU seconds to run on a 64C64T server, but only 140 CPU seconds on a 4C8T laptop. So it looks like seneschal wastes a lot of CPU time coordinating the CPUs.

Compare this to:

echo 1 | time -v parallel -N0 'yes `seq 1000`|head -c 1G' >/dev/null

which takes 3-8 CPU seconds.

EXAMPLES FROM seneschal README.md

1$ echo $REPOS | seneschal --prefix="cd {} && git pull"
# If $REPOS is newline separated
1$ echo "$REPOS" | parallel -k "cd {} && git pull"
# If $REPOS is space separated
1$ echo -n "$REPOS" | parallel -d' ' -k "cd {} && git pull"
COMMANDS="pwd
sleep 5 && echo boom
echo Howdy
whoami"

2$ echo "$COMMANDS" | seneschal --debug

2$ echo "$COMMANDS" | parallel -k -v

3$ ls -1 | seneschal --prefix="pushd {}; git pull; popd;"

3$ ls -1 | parallel -k "pushd {}; git pull; popd;"
# Or if current dir also contains files:
3$ parallel -k "pushd {}; git pull; popd;" ::: */

https://github.com/TheWizardTower/seneschal (Last checked: 2022-06)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN async AND GNU Parallel

Summary (see legend above):

x x x x x x x
- x x x x x
x O2 O3 O4 O5 O6 - x x O10
E1 - - E4 - - -
- - - - - - - - -
S1 S2

async works like sem.

EXAMPLES FROM async

1$ S="/tmp/example_socket"
   async -s="$S" server --start
   for i in {1..20}; do
       # prints command output to stdout
       async -s="$S" cmd -- bash -c "sleep 1 && echo test $i"
   done

   # wait until all commands are finished
   async -s="$S" wait

1$ S="example_id"
   # server not needed
   for i in {1..20}; do
       # prints command output to stdout
       sem --bg --id "$S" -j100% "sleep 1 && echo test $i"
   done

   # wait until all commands are finished
   sem --fg --id "$S" --wait

2$ # configure the server to run four commands in parallel
   async -s="$S" server -j4

   mkdir "/tmp/ex_dir"
   for i in {21..40}; do
       # redirects command output to /tmp/ex_dir/file*
       async -s="$S" cmd -o "/tmp/ex_dir/file$i" -- \
         bash -c "sleep 1 && echo test $i"

https://github.com/MikaelE86/async (Last checked: 2024-06)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE xargs Y GNU Parallel

Resumen (ver leyenda arriba):

I1 - - - - - -
M1 - M3 - - M6
O1 - O3 O4 - - - x x -
E1 - - - - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

GNU Parallel es un paralelizador general y un reemplazo directo para xargs.

Esto significa que todas las invocaciones de xargs se pueden reemplazar por GNU Parallel.

find . -name '*.jpg' | xargs -n 1 libexif-tools/exiftool
find . -name '*.jpg' | parallel -n 1 libexif-tools/exiftool

GNU Parallel suele ser más rápido que xargs.

seq 10000 | xargs sleep
seq 10000 | parallel --n 1 sleep

GNU Parallel también puede ejecutar trabajos en segundo plano y cerrarlos explícitamente:

seq 10000 | parallel --tag --joblog joblog.txt sleep

GNU Parallel también puede iniciar trabajos por argumentos en lugar de mediante tuberías:

parallel --tag --joblog joblog.txt sleep ::: $(seq 10000)

GNU Parallel también puede iniciar trabajos en hosts SSH remotos:

parallel --tag --onall 'ssh user@host "hostname"'

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE GNU Parallel Y parallel

Resumen (ver leyenda arriba):

- - - - - M6
- O2 O3 - O5 O6 - x x -
E1 - - - - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

La utilidad parallel de GNU Parallel es la herramienta original. Hay una nueva versión de GNU Parallel (ver GNU Parallel arriba) que tiene mucha más funcionalidad.

1$ seq 5 | parallel echo {}
1$ parallel echo ::: $(seq 5)

- - - - - M6

- - - - - - - - -
- -

loom es un script de shell que se utiliza para ejecutar tareas en paralelo.

No es tan flexible como GNU Parallel.

1$ loom -c 4 -m 1000 echo {1..5}
1$ parallel -j 4 --memfree 1000M echo {1..5}

https://github.com/joel/loom (Última comprobación: 2024-05)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE GNU Parallel Y parallel

Resumen (ver leyenda arriba):

- -

La utilidad parallel es un script de shell que ejecuta comandos en paralelo. GNU Parallel es una alternativa más rica en funcionalidades.

1$ parallel echo {1..5}

GNU Parallel puede hacer lo mismo:

1$ parallel echo ::: {1..5}

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE GNU Parallel Y SHELL

Resumen (ver leyenda arriba):

I1 I2 - - - - (I7)
- - M3 - - M6
- O2 O3 - O5 - - x x -
E1 - - E4 - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

GNU Parallel es un paralelizador general y un reemplazo directo para las tuberías de shell.

ls *.jpg | xargs -n 1 libexif-tools/exiftool
ls *.jpg | parallel -n 1 libexif-tools/exiftool

GNU Parallel suele ser más rápido que las tuberías de shell.

seq 10000 | xargs sleep
seq 10000 | parallel --n 1 sleep

GNU Parallel también puede ejecutar trabajos en segundo plano y cerrarlos explícitamente:

seq 10000 | parallel --tag --joblog joblog.txt sleep

GNU Parallel también puede iniciar trabajos por argumentos en lugar de mediante tuberías:

parallel --tag --joblog joblog.txt sleep ::: $(seq 10000)

GNU Parallel también puede iniciar trabajos en hosts SSH remotos:

parallel --tag --onall 'ssh user@host "hostname"'

- -

mk es una herramienta para ejecutar comandos en paralelo. Es similar a GNU Parallel pero menos flexible.

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE go-pssh Y GNU Parallel

Resumen (ver leyenda arriba):

- - - - -
O1 - - - - - - x x O10
E1 - - - - - -
R1 R2 - - - R6 - - -
- -

go-pssh ejecuta ssh en paralelo a múltiples máquinas. Ejecuta el mismo comando en múltiples máquinas, similar a --nonall.

Los nombres de host deben proporcionarse como direcciones IP (no como nombres de host).

La salida se envía a stdout (salida estándar) si el comando es exitoso, y a stderr (error estándar) si el comando falla.

EJEMPLOS DE go-pssh

1$ go-pssh -l <ip>,<ip> -u <user> -p <port> -P <passwd> -c "<command>"
1$ parallel -S 'sshpass -p <passwd> ssh -p <port> <user>@<ip>' \
     --nonall "<command>"
2$ go-pssh scp -f host.txt -u <user> -p <port> -P <password> \
     -s /local/file_or_directory -d /remote/directory
2$ parallel --nonall --slf host.txt \
     --basefile /local/file_or_directory/./ --wd /remote/directory
     --ssh 'sshpass -p <password> ssh -p <port> -l <user>' true
3$ go-pssh scp -l <ip>,<ip> -u <user> -p <port> -P <password> \
     -s /local/file_or_directory -d /remote/directory
3$ parallel --nonall -S <ip>,<ip> \
     --basefile /local/file_or_directory/./ --wd /remote/directory
     --ssh 'sshpass -p <password> ssh -p <port> -l <user>' true

https://github.com/xuchenCN/go-pssh (Última comprobación: 2021-07)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE go-parallel Y GNU Parallel

Resumen (ver leyenda arriba):

I1 I2 - - - - (I7)
- - M3 - - M6
- O2 O3 - O5 - - x x -
E1 - - E4 - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

go-parallel utiliza plantillas Go para las cadenas de reemplazo. Bastante similar a la cadena de reemplazo {= perl expr =}.

Las cadenas de reemplazo básicas se pueden emular colocando esto en ~/.parallel/config:

--rpl '{{.Input}} '
--rpl '{{.Time}} use DateTime; $_= DateTime->from_epoch(time);'
--rpl '{{.Start}} use DateTime; $_= DateTime->from_epoch($^T);'

Entonces puedes hacer:

seq 10 | parallel sleep {{.Input}}';' echo {{.Start}} {{.Time}}
seq 10 | go-parallel -t 'bash -c "sleep {{.Input}}; echo \"{{.Start}}\" \"{{.Time}}\""'

Si la entrada es demasiado larga (64K), no obtienes error:

perl -e 'print "works."."x"x100' | parallel.go -t 'echo {{noExt .Input}} '
perl -e 'print "fails."."x"x100_000_000' | parallel.go -t 'echo {{noExt .Input}} '

Los caracteres especiales se citan:

echo '"&+<>' | go-parallel echo
"&+<>

pero no se citan en el shell cuando se usan cadenas de reemplazo:

echo '"&+<>' | go-parallel -t 'echo {{.Input}}'
&#34;&amp;&#43;&lt;&gt;

EJEMPLOS DE go-parallel

1$ go-parallel -a ./files.txt -t 'cp {{.Input}} {{.Input | dirname | dirname}}'
1$ parallel -a ./files.txt cp {} '{= $_=::dirname(::dirname($_)) =}'
2$ go-parallel -a ./files.txt -t 'mkdir -p {{.Input}} {{noExt .Input}}'
2$ parallel -a ./files.txt echo mkdir -p {} {.}
3$ go-parallel -a ./files.txt -t 'mkdir -p {{.Input}} {{.Input | basename | noExt}}'
3$ parallel -a ./files.txt echo mkdir -p {} {/.}
4$ time find ~/src/go -type f | go-parallel md5sum > /dev/null
4$ time find ~/src/go -type f | parallel md5sum > /dev/null
   # Aunque probablemente harías esto en su lugar:
   time find ~/src/go -type f | parallel -X md5sum > /dev/null

https://github.com/mylanconnolly/parallel (Última comprobación: 2024-06)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE p Y GNU Parallel

Resumen (ver leyenda arriba):

- - - I4 - - x
- - - - - M6
- O2 O3 - O5 O6 - x x -
E1 - - - - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

p es un pequeño script de shell. Puede colorear la salida con algunos colores predefinidos, pero por lo demás es bastante limitado.

Alcanza un máximo de alrededor de 116000 tareas (probablemente debido a limitaciones en Bash).

EJEMPLOS DE p

Algunos de los ejemplos de p no se pueden implementar al 100% con GNU parallel: el coloreado es un poco diferente, y GNU parallel no puede tener --tag para algunas entradas y no para otras.

El coloreado realizado por GNU parallel no es exactamente el mismo que p.

1$ p -bc blue "ping 127.0.0.1" -uc red "ping 192.168.0.1" \
   -rc yellow "ping 192.168.1.1" -t example "ping example.com"
1$ parallel --lb -j0 --color --tag ping \
   ::: 127.0.0.1 192.168.0.1 192.168.1.1 example.com
2$ p "tail -f /var/log/httpd/access_log" \
   -bc red "tail -f /var/log/httpd/error_log"
2$ cd /var/log/httpd;
   parallel --lb --color --tag tail -f ::: access_log error_log
3$ p tail -f "some file" \& p tail -f "other file with space.txt"
3$ parallel --lb tail -f ::: 'some file' "other file with space.txt"
4$ p -t project1 "hg pull project1" -t project2 \
   "hg pull project2" -t project3 "hg pull project3"
4$ parallel --lb hg pull ::: project{1..3}

https://github.com/rudymatela/evenmoreutils/blob/master/man/p.1.adoc (Última comprobación: 2022-04)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE senechal Y GNU Parallel

Resumen (ver leyenda arriba):

I1 - - - - - -
M1 - M3 - - M6
O1 - O3 O4 - - - x x -
E1 - - - - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

seneschal solo inicia la primera tarea después de leer la última tarea, y la salida de la primera tarea solo se imprime después de que la última tarea finaliza.

1 byte de salida requiere 3.5 bytes de RAM.

Esto hace imposible tener una salida total mayor que la memoria virtual.

Aunque la salida se mantiene en RAM, la salida es bastante lenta: 30 MB/s.

Una salida mayor de 4 GB causa problemas aleatorios; parece una condición de carrera.

Esto:

echo 1 | seneschal  --prefix='yes `seq 1000`|head -c 1G' >/dev/null

toma 4100(!) segundos de CPU para ejecutarse en un servidor 64C64T, pero solo 140 segundos de CPU en un portátil 4C8T. Por lo tanto, parece que seneschal desperdicia mucho tiempo de CPU coordinando las CPUs.

Compárelo con:

echo 1 | time -v parallel -N0 'yes `seq 1000`|head -c 1G' >/dev/null

que toma 3-8 segundos de CPU.

EJEMPLOS DEL README.md DE seneschal

1$ echo $REPOS | seneschal --prefix="cd {} && git pull"
# Si $REPOS está separado por saltos de línea
1$ echo "$REPOS" | parallel -k "cd {} && git pull"
# Si $REPOS está separado por espacios
1$ echo -n "$REPOS" | parallel -d' ' -k "cd {} && git pull"
COMMANDS="pwd
sleep 5 && echo boom
echo Howdy
whoami"

2$ echo "$COMMANDS" | seneschal --debug

2$ echo "$COMMANDS" | parallel -k -v

3$ ls -1 | seneschal --prefix="pushd {}; git pull; popd;"

3$ ls -1 | parallel -k "pushd {}; git pull; popd;"
# O si el directorio actual también contiene archivos:
3$ parallel -k "pushd {}; git pull; popd;" ::: */

https://github.com/TheWizardTower/seneschal (Última comprobación: 2022-06)

DIFERENCIAS ENTRE async Y GNU Parallel

Resumen (ver leyenda arriba):

x x x x x x x
- x x x x x
x O2 O3 O4 O5 O6 - x x O10
E1 - - E4 - - -
- - - - - - - - -
S1 S2

async funciona como sem.

EJEMPLOS DE async

1$ S="/tmp/example_socket"
   async -s="$S" server --start
   for i in {1..20}; do
       # imprime la salida del comando en stdout
       async -s="$S" cmd -- bash -c "sleep 1 && echo test $i"
   done

   # espera hasta que todos los comandos hayan terminado
   async -s="$S" wait

1$ S="example_id"
   # no se necesita servidor
   for i in {1..20}; do
       # imprime la salida del comando en stdout
       sem --bg --id "$S" -j100% "sleep 1 && echo test $i"
   done

   # espera hasta que todos los comandos hayan terminado
   sem --fg --id "$S" --wait

2$ # configura el servidor para ejecutar cuatro comandos en paralelo
   async -s="$S" server -j4

   mkdir "/tmp/ex_dir"
   for i in {21..40}; do
       # redirige la salida del comando a /tmp/ex_dir/file*
       async -s="$S" cmd -o "/tmp/ex_dir/file$i" -- \
         bash -c "sleep 1 && echo test $i"

https://github.com/MikaelE86/async (Última comprobación: 2024-06)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN async AND GNU Parallel

Summary (see legend above):

I1 I2 I3 - - - -
- - - - - M6
O1 O2 O3 - O5 O6 - - - O10
E1 - - E4 - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

async is a POSIX shell utility to run commands in parallel. It is a part of the async package. It is similar to GNU parallel but without some of the advanced features like job replacement, argument shuffling, file splitting, remote execution, job server, etc. async is designed to be simpler and easier to use.

The main differences are:

  • async does not have a job server. Each command is executed in a separate process.
  • async does not support job replacement, argument shuffling, file splitting, or remote execution.
  • async is designed to be simpler and easier to use than GNU parallel.

EXAMPLES

# Starting a server and running jobs against it
$ S=my_server_id
$ async -s=$S server --start echo hi
$ async -s=$S client --timeout 10 echo hello
$ async -s=$S server --stop

# Running jobs in background and collecting results
$ mkdir /tmp/ex_dir
$ for i in {21..40}; do
      async -s=$S --results /tmp/ex_dir/file$i{} \
        "sleep 1 && echo test $i"
  done
$ async -s=$S --wait
$ async -s=$S server --stop

# Starting server not needed
$ mkdir /tmp/ex_dir
$ for i in {21..40}; do
      # redirects command output to /tmp/ex_dir/file*
      async --bg --id $S --results /tmp/ex_dir/file$i{} \
        "sleep 1 && echo test $i"
  done
$ async --fg --id $S --wait
# there is no server to stop

https://github.com/ctbur/async (Last checked: 2023-01)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN tandem AND GNU Parallel

Summary (see legend above):

- - - I4 - - x
M1 - - - - M6
- - O3 - - - - x - -
E1 - E3 - E5 - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

tandem runs full commands in parallel. It is made for starting a "server", running a job against the server, and when the job is done, the server is killed.

More generally: it kills all jobs when the first job completes - similar to '--halt now,done=1'.

tandem silently discards some output. It is unclear exactly when this happens. It looks like a race condition, because it varies for each run.

$ tandem "seq 10000" | wc -l
6731 <- This should always be 10002

EXAMPLES FROM Demo

tandem \
  'php -S localhost:8000' \
  'esbuild src/*.ts --bundle --outdir=dist --watch' \
  'tailwind -i src/index.css -o dist/index.css --watch'
# Emulate tandem's behaviour
PARALLEL='--color --lb  --halt now,done=1 --tagstring '
PARALLEL="$PARALLEL'"'{=s/ .*//; $_.=".".$app{$_}++;=}'"'"
export PARALLEL
parallel ::: \
  'php -S localhost:8000' \
  'esbuild src/*.ts --bundle --outdir=dist --watch' \
  'tailwind -i src/index.css -o dist/index.css --watch'

EXAMPLES FROM tandem -h

# Emulate tandem's behaviour
PARALLEL='--color --lb  --halt now,done=1 --tagstring '
PARALLEL="$PARALLEL'"'{=s/ .*//; $_.=".".$app{$_}++;=}'"'"
export PARALLEL
1$ tandem 'sleep 5 && echo "hello"' 'sleep 2 && echo "world"'
1$ parallel ::: 'sleep 5 && echo "hello"' 'sleep 2 && echo "world"'
# '-t 0' fails. But '--timeout 0 works'
2$ tandem --timeout 0 'sleep 5 && echo "hello"' \
     'sleep 2 && echo "world"'
2$ parallel --timeout 0 ::: 'sleep 5 && echo "hello"' \
     'sleep 2 && echo "world"'

EXAMPLES FROM tandem's readme.md

# Emulate tandem's behaviour
PARALLEL='--color --lb  --halt now,done=1 --tagstring '
PARALLEL="$PARALLEL'"'{=s/ .*//; $_.=".".$app{$_}++;=}'"'"
export PARALLEL
1$ tandem 'next dev' 'nodemon --quiet ./server.js'
1$ parallel ::: 'next dev' 'nodemon --quiet ./server.js'
2$ cat package.json
   {
     "scripts": {
       "dev:php": "...",
       "dev:js": "...",
       "dev:css": "..."
     }
   }
   tandem 'npm:dev:php' 'npm:dev:js' 'npm:dev:css'
# GNU Parallel uses bash functions instead
2$ cat package.sh
   dev:php() { ... ; }
   dev:js() { ... ; }
   dev:css() { ... ; }
   export -f dev:php dev:js dev:css
   . package.sh
   parallel ::: dev:php dev:js dev:css
3$ tandem 'npm:dev:*'
3$ compgen -A function | grep ^dev: | parallel

For usage in Makefiles, include a copy of GNU Parallel with your source using `parallel --embed`. This has the added benefit of also working if access to the internet is down or restricted.

https://github.com/rosszurowski/tandem (Last checked: 2023-01)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN rust-parallel(aaronriekenberg) AND GNU Parallel

Summary (see legend above):

I1 I2 I3 - - - -
- - - - - M6
O1 O2 O3 - O5 O6 - x - O10
E1 - - E4 - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

rust-parallel has a goal of only using Rust. It seems it is impossible to call bash functions from the command line. You would need to put these in a script.

Calling a script that misses the shebang line (#! as first line) fails.

EXAMPLES FROM rust-parallel's README.md

$ cat >./test <<EOL
echo hi
echo there
echo how
echo are
echo you
EOL

1$ cat test | rust-parallel -j5

1$ cat test | parallel -j5

2$ cat test | rust-parallel -j1

2$ cat test | parallel -j1

3$ head -100 /usr/share/dict/words | rust-parallel md5 -s

3$ head -100 /usr/share/dict/words | parallel md5 -s

4$ find . -type f -print0 | rust-parallel -0 gzip -f -k

4$ find . -type f -print0 | parallel -0 gzip -f -k

5$ head -100 /usr/share/dict/words |
     awk '{printf "md5 -s %s\n", $1}' | rust-parallel

5$ head -100 /usr/share/dict/words |
     awk '{printf "md5 -s %s\n", $1}' | parallel

6$ head -100 /usr/share/dict/words | rust-parallel md5 -s |
     grep -i abba

6$ head -100 /usr/share/dict/words | parallel md5 -s |
     grep -i abba

https://github.com/aaronriekenberg/rust-parallel (Last checked: 2023-01)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN parallelium AND GNU Parallel

Summary (see legend above):

- I2 - - - - -
M1 - - - - M6
O1 - O3 - - - - x - -
E1 - - E4 - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

parallelium merges standard output (stdout) and standard error (stderr). The maximal output of a command is 8192 bytes. Bigger output makes parallelium go into an infinite loop.

In the input file for parallelium you can define a tag, so that you can select to run only these commands. A bit like a target in a Makefile.

Progress is printed on standard output (stdout) prepended with '#' with similar information as GNU parallel's --bar.

EXAMPLES

$ cat testjobs.txt
#tag common sleeps classA
(sleep 4.495;echo "job 000")
:
(sleep 2.587;echo "job 016")

#tag common sleeps classB
(sleep 0.218;echo "job 017")
:
(sleep 2.269;echo "job 040")

#tag common sleeps classC
(sleep 2.586;echo "job 041")
:
(sleep 1.626;echo "job 099")

#tag lasthalf, sleeps, classB
(sleep 1.540;echo "job 100")
:
(sleep 2.001;echo "job 199")
1$ parallelium -f testjobs.txt -l logdir -t classB,classC
1$ cat testjobs.txt |
     parallel --plus --results logdir/testjobs.txt_{0#}.output \
       '{= if(/^#tag /) { @tag = split/,|\s+/ }
           (grep /^(classB|classC)$/, @tag) or skip =}'

https://github.com/beomagi/parallelium (Last checked: 2023-01)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN forkrun AND GNU Parallel

Summary (see legend above):

I1 - - - - - I7
- - - - - -
- O2 O3 - O5 - - - - O10
E1 - - E4 - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

forkrun blocks if it receives fewer jobs than slots:

echo | forkrun -p 2 echo

or when it gets some specific commands e.g.:

f() { seq "$@" | pv -qL 3; }
seq 10 | forkrun f

It is not clear why.

It is faster than GNU parallel (overhead: 1.2 ms/job vs 3 ms/job), but way slower than parallel-bash (0.059 ms/job).

Running jobs cannot be stopped by pressing CTRL-C.

-k is supposed to keep the order but fails on the MIX testing example below. If used with -k it caches output in RAM.

If forkrun is killed, it leaves temporary files in /tmp/.forkrun.* that has to be cleaned up manually.

EXAMPLES

1$ time find ./ -type f |
     forkrun -l512 -- sha256sum 2>/dev/null | wc -l
1$ time find ./ -type f |
     parallel -j28 -m -- sha256sum 2>/dev/null | wc -l
2$ time find ./ -type f |
     forkrun -l512 -k -- sha256sum 2>/dev/null | wc -l
2$ time find ./ -type f |
     parallel -j28 -k -m -- sha256sum 2>/dev/null | wc -l

https://github.com/jkool702/forkrun (Last checked: 2023-02)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN parallel-sh AND GNU Parallel

Summary (see legend above):

I1 I2 - I4 - - -
M1 - - - - M6
O1 O2 O3 - O5 O6 - - - O10
E1 - - E4 - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

parallel-sh buffers in RAM. The buffering data takes O(n^1.5) time:

2MB=0.107s 4MB=0.175s 8MB=0.342s 16MB=0.766s 32MB=2.2s 64MB=6.7s 128MB=20s 256MB=64s 512MB=248s 1024MB=998s 2048MB=3756s

It limits the practical usability to jobs outputting < 256 MB. GNU parallel buffers on disk, yet is faster for jobs with outputs > 16 MB and is only limited by the free space in $TMPDIR.

parallel-sh can kill running jobs if a job fails (Similar to --halt now,fail=1).

EXAMPLES

1$ parallel-sh "sleep 2 && echo first" "
parallel-sh(1) - Linux manual page

NAME

parallel-sh - execute commands in parallel

SYNOPSIS

parallel-sh [-j num_jobs] [-f file] [-a file] [--args-sep char] [--results dir] [--tag] [command ...]

parallel [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --pipe [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --pipepart [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --keeporder [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --results [DIR] [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --slurp [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --slurpfile [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout.kill [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout.terminate [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout.signal [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout.default [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout.signal.default [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout.kill.default [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout.terminate.default [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout.terminating.signal [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout.terminating.kill [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout.terminating.terminate [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout.terminating.default [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout.terminating.signal.default [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout.terminating.kill.default [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout.terminating.terminate.default [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout.term.signal [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout.term.kill [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout.term.terminate [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout.term.default [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout.term.signal.default [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout.term.kill.default [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout.term.terminate.default [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout.timeout [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout.timeout.signal [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout.timeout.kill [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout.timeout.terminate [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout.timeout.default [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout.timeout.signal.default [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout.timeout.kill.default [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --timeout.timeout.terminate.default [OPTIONS] [COMMAND [ARG ...]]

parallel --citation print citation details and exit

parallel --help print help message and exit

parallel --version print version information and exit

DESCRIPTION

GNU Parallel is a general parallelizer that combines multiple CPU cores and machines for faster program execution.

It is recommended to read the tutorial:

    parallel --citation

This manual page provides a summary of the most common options.

The options parallel-sh and parallel are very similar. parallel-sh is a simple wrapper around parallel which reads commands from standard input, one command per line.

parallel reads commands from standard input if no commands are given on the command line.

The commands are executed in parallel, and the output is printed to standard output.

By default, parallel uses the number of CPU cores available as the number of parallel jobs. This can be changed with the -j option.

parallel supports remote execution using SSH.

Placeholder replacement is supported using {}, {.}, {/}, {%}, {= ... =}, etc.

parallel supports job control, progress output, job logging, and much more.

See the examples section for common use cases.

OPTIONS

A full list of options can be found in the online documentation at https://www.gnu.org/software/parallel/.

-j num_jobs, --jobs num_jobs
Specifies the number of jobs to run in parallel. Defaults to the number of CPU cores.
-f file, --files file
Reads commands from the specified file. This is equivalent to cat file | parallel.
-a file, --arg-file file
Reads arguments from the specified file. Each line in the file is treated as a separate argument.
--args-sep char
Specifies the separator character for arguments read from a file. Defaults to newline.
--results dir
Stores the results of each job in the specified directory. Each job's output is stored in a separate file within this directory.
--tag
Prepends the output of each job with the command that generated it.
--pipe
Reads commands from standard input and pipes the output of each command to the next command in the pipeline.
--pipepart
Similar to --pipe, but splits the input into parts and processes them in parallel.
--keeporder
Ensures that the output is printed in the same order as the input commands.
--slurp
Reads all input into memory before processing.
--slurpfile
Reads all input from a file into memory before processing.
--timeout seconds
Sets a timeout for each job. If a job exceeds the timeout, it will be killed.
--citation
Prints citation details and exits.
--help
Prints a help message and exits.
--version
Prints version information and exits.

EXAMPLES

Run two commands in parallel, each with a different sleep time:

1$ parallel ::: "sleep 2 && echo first" "sleep 1 && echo second"
second
first

Read commands from a file:

2$ cat /tmp/commands
   sleep 2 && echo first
   sleep 1 && echo second
2$ parallel -f /tmp/commands
second
first
2$ parallel -a /tmp/commands
second
first

Read commands from standard input via a pipe:

3$ echo -e 'sleep 2 && echo first\nsleep 1 && echo second' |
     parallel-sh
second
first
3$ echo -e 'sleep 2 && echo first\nsleep 1 && echo second' |
     parallel
second
first

SEE ALSO

GNU Parallel manual (https://www.gnu.org/software/parallel/)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN parallel-sh AND GNU Parallel

Summary (see legend above):

- I2 - - - - I7
M1 - M3 - M5 M6
- O2 O3 - - O6 - O8 - O10
E1 - - - - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

parallel-sh is not as much a command as it is a shell script that you have to alter. It requires you to change the shell function process_job that runs the job, and set $MAX_POOL_SIZE to the number of jobs to run in parallel.

It is half as fast as GNU parallel for short jobs.

https://github.com/thyrc/parallel-sh (Last checked: 2023-04)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN bash-parallel AND GNU Parallel

Summary (see legend above):

- I2 - - - - I7
M1 - M3 - M5 M6
- O2 O3 - - O6 - O8 - O10
E1 - - - - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

bash-parallel is not as much a command as it is a shell script that you have to alter. It requires you to change the shell function process_job that runs the job, and set $MAX_POOL_SIZE to the number of jobs to run in parallel.

It is half as fast as GNU parallel for short jobs.

https://github.com/thilinaba/bash-parallel (Last checked: 2024-06)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN PaSH AND GNU Parallel

Summary (see legend above): N/A

pash is quite different from GNU parallel. It is not a general parallelizer. It takes a shell script and analyses it and parallelizes parts of it by replacing the parts with commands that will give the same result.

This will replace sort with a command that does pretty much the same as parsort --parallel=8 (except somewhat slower):

pa.sh --width 8 -c 'cat bigfile | sort'

However, even a simple change will confuse pash and you will get no parallelization:

pa.sh --width 8 -c 'mysort() { sort; }; cat bigfile | mysort'
pa.sh --width 8 -c 'cat bigfile | sort | md5sum'

From the source it seems pash only looks at: awk cat col comm cut diff grep head mkfifo mv rm sed seq sort tail tee tr uniq wc xargs

For pipelines where these commands are bottlenecks, it might be worth testing if pash is faster than GNU parallel.

pash does not respect $TMPDIR but always uses /tmp. If pash dies unexpectantly it does not clean up.

https://github.com/binpash/pash (Last checked: 2023-05)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN korovkin-parallel AND GNU Parallel

Summary (see legend above):

I1 - - - - - -
M1 - - - - M6
- - O3 - - - - x x -
E1 - - - - - -
R1 - - - - R6 x x -
- -

korovkin-parallel prepends all lines with some info.

The output is colored with 6 color combinations, so job 1 and 7 will get the same color.

You can get similar output with:

(echo ...) |
  parallel --color -j 10 --lb --tagstring \
    '[l:{#}:{=$_=sprintf("%7.03f",::now()-$^T)=} {=$_=hh_mm_ss($^T)=} {%}]'

Lines longer than 8192 chars are broken into lines shorter than 8192. korovkin-parallel loses the last char for lines exactly 8193 chars long.

Short lines from different jobs do not mix, but long lines do:

fun() {
  perl -e '$a="'$1'"x1000000; for(1..'$2') { print $a };';
  echo;
}
export -f fun
(echo fun a 100;echo fun b 100) | korovkin-parallel | tr -s abcdef
# Compare to:
(echo fun a 100;echo fun b 100) | parallel | tr -s abcdef

There should be only one line of a's and one line of b's.

Just like GNU parallel korovkin-parallel offers a master/slave model, so workers on other servers can do some of the tasks. But contrary to GNU parallel you must manually start workers on these servers. The communication is neither authenticated nor encrypted.

It caches output in RAM: a 1GB line uses ~2.5GB RAM

https://github.com/korovkin/parallel (Last checked: 2023-07)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN xe AND GNU Parallel

Summary (see legend above):

I1 I2 - I4 - - I7
M1 - M3 M4 - M6
- O2 O3 - O5 O6 - O8 - O10
E1 - - E4 - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

xe has a peculiar limitation:

echo /bin/echo | xe {} OK
echo echo | xe /bin/{} fails

EXAMPLES

Compress all .c files in the current directory, using all CPU cores:

1$ xe -a -j0 gzip -- *.c
1$ parallel gzip ::: *.c

Remove all empty files, using lr(1):

2$ lr -U -t 'size == 0' | xe -N0 rm
2$ lr -U -t 'size == 0' | parallel -X rm

Convert .mp3 to .ogg, using all CPU cores:

3$ xe -a -j0 -s 'ffmpeg -i "${1}" "${1%.mp3}.ogg"' -- *.mp3
3$ parallel ffmpeg -i {} {.}.ogg ::: *.mp3

Same, using percent rules:

4$ xe -a -j0 -p %.mp3 ffmpeg -i %.mp3 %.ogg -- *.mp3
4$ parallel --rpl '% s/\.mp3// or skip' ffmpeg -i %.mp3 %.ogg ::: *.mp3

Similar, but hiding output of ffmpeg, instead showing spawned jobs:

5$ xe -ap -j0 -vvq '%.{m4a,ogg,opus}' ffmpeg -y -i {} out/%.mp3 -- *
5$ parallel -v --rpl '% s/\.(m4a|ogg|opus)// or skip' \
     ffmpeg -y -i {} out/%.mp3 '2>/dev/null' ::: *
5$ parallel -v ffmpeg -y -i {} out/{.}.mp3 '2>/dev/null' ::: *

https://github.com/leahneukirchen/xe (Last checked: 2023-08)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN sp AND GNU Parallel

Summary (see legend above):

- - - I4 - - -
M1 - M3 - - M6
- O2 O3 - O5 (O6) - x x O10
E1 - - - - - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

sp has very few options.

It can either be used like:

sp command {} option :: arg1 arg2 arg3

which is similar to:

parallel command {} option ::: arg1 arg2 arg3

Or:

sp command1 :: "command2 -option" :: "command3 foo bar"

which is similar to:

parallel ::: command1 "command2 -option" "command3 foo bar"

sp deals badly with too many commands: This causes sp to run out of file handles and gives data loss.

For each command that fails, sp will print an error message on stderr (standard error).

You cannot used exported shell functions as commands.

EXAMPLES

1$ sp echo {} :: 1 2 3
1$ parallel echo {} ::: 1 2 3
2$ sp echo {} {} :: 1 2 3
2$ parallel echo {} {} :: 1 2 3
3$ sp echo 1 :: echo 2 :: echo 3
3$ parallel ::: 'echo 1' 'echo 2' 'echo 3'
4$ sp a foo bar :: "b 'baz  bar'" :: c
4$ parallel ::: 'a foo bar' "b 'baz  bar'" :: c

https://github.com/SergioBenitez/sp (Last checked: 2023-10)

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN repeater AND GNU Parallel

Summary (see legend above):

- - - - - - -
- - - - - -
- O2 O3 x - O6 - x x ?O10
E1 - - - E5 - -
- - - - - - - - -
- -

repeater runs the same job repeatedly. In other words: It does not read arguments, thus is it an alternative for GNU parallel for only quite limited applications.

repeater has an overhead of around 0.23 ms/job. Compared to GNU parallel's 2-3 ms this is fast. Compared to bash-parallel's 0.05 ms/job it is slow.

Memory use and run time for large output

Output takes O(n^2) time for output of size n. 10 MB takes ~1 second, 30 MB takes ~7 seconds, 100 MB takes ~60 seconds, 300 MB takes ~480 seconds, 1000 MB takes ~10000 seconds.

100 MB of output takes around 1 GB of RAM.

# Run time = 15 sec
# Memory use = 20 MB
# Output = 1 GB per job
\time -v parallel -j1 seq ::: 120000000 120000000 >/dev/null
# Run time = 4.7 sec
# Memory use = 95 MB
# Output = 8 MB per job
\time -v repeater -w 1 -n 2 -reportFile ./run_output seq 1200000 >/dev/null
# Run time = 42 sec
# Memory use = 277 MB
# Output = 27 MB per job
\time -v repeater -w 1 -n 2 -reportFile ./run_output seq 3600000 >/dev/null
# Run time = 530 sec
# Memory use = 1000 MB
# Output = 97 MB per job
\time -v repeater -w 1 -n 2 -reportFile ./run_output seq 12000000 >/dev/null
# Run time = 2h41m
# Memory use = 8.6 GB
# Output = 1 GB per job
\time -v repeater -w 1 -n 2 -reportFile ./run_output seq 120000000 >/dev/null

For even just moderate sized outputs GNU parallel will be faster and use less memory.

EXAMPLES

1$ repeater -n 100 -w 10 -reportFile ./run_output
     -output REPORT_FILE -progress BOTH curl example.com
1$ seq 100 | parallel --joblog run.log --eta curl exa

NAME

parallel - Build and execute command lines from standard input in parallel

SYNOPSIS

parallel [OPTION]... [COMMAND [ARG]...]...

parallel --citation [OPTION]...

DESCRIPTION

GNU parallel is a tool for executing jobs in parallel using all of the available cores and machines. It is a general parallelization tool that can be used for tasks that can be parallelized, such as processing files, running commands on multiple hosts, and more.

It can be used to replace shell loops, xargs, find, and more, with a simpler and more powerful syntax.

OPTIONS AND EXAMPLES

GNU parallel supports a wide range of options and features. Here are some common use cases and examples:

BASIC USAGE: Run a command on each line of input

To run a command on each line of input, you can use the `{}` placeholder, which will be replaced by the line of input.

echo "file1.txt file2.txt" | parallel wc -l {}

This will run `wc -l file1.txt` and `wc -l file2.txt` in parallel.

ADVANCED USAGE: Handling command line arguments

GNU parallel provides various replacement strings for more complex argument handling:

  • `{}`: The entire input line.
  • `{.}`: The input line without the extension.
  • `{/}`: The basename of the input line.
  • `{}`: The dirname of the input line.
  • `{#}`: The job number.
ls *.txt | parallel --tag --results resultsdir mv {} processed/{/}

This command renames all `.txt` files to have a `processed/` prefix, using the original filename as the base. The `--tag` option prints the input line before the output of the command, and `--results` saves the output in a specified directory.

JOB CONTROL: Controlling the number of parallel jobs

You can control the number of jobs running in parallel using the `-j` option.

seq 100 | parallel -j 4 echo Processing item {}

This will run at most 4 jobs in parallel.

seq 100 | parallel -j 0 echo Processing item {}

Using `-j 0` will run as many jobs in parallel as there are CPU cores.

REMOTE EXECUTION: Running jobs on remote machines

GNU parallel can execute jobs on remote machines using SSH.

parallel --onall --user user --hosts host1,host2 'echo {}' ::: 1 2 3

This runs `echo 1`, `echo 2`, and `echo 3` on `host1` and `host2` in parallel.

OUTPUT CONTROL: Managing output and errors

GNU parallel offers fine-grained control over output and error handling.

seq 5 | parallel --results outputdir --joblog job.log 'sleep {}; echo "Job {} done"'

This example runs 5 jobs, each sleeping for a random amount of time, and then prints a completion message. The `--results` option saves the output of each job into separate files in the `outputdir` directory, and `--joblog` logs the status of each job.

PREDICATE COMPATIBILITY: Mimicking other tools

GNU parallel provides options to mimic the behavior of other tools like `xargs` and `find`.

XARGS COMPATIBILITY: Handling arguments like xargs

The `--xargs` option allows GNU parallel to accept arguments in a similar way to `xargs`.

find . -name "*.log" | parallel --xargs rm

This is equivalent to `find . -name "*.log" -delete` but runs `rm` in parallel.

FIND COMPATIBILITY: Executing commands on find results

GNU parallel can be used to execute commands found by `find`.

find . -type f -print0 | parallel -0 chmod 644

This command finds all files (`-type f`) and executes `chmod 644` on them in parallel. The `-print0` and `-0` options handle filenames with special characters safely.

RESOURCE MANAGEMENT: Handling large outputs and memory

GNU parallel is designed to handle large inputs and outputs efficiently, without exhausting system resources.

MEMFULL: Large output exceeding physical memory

GNU parallel can handle outputs that are larger than physical memory, preventing crashes that might occur with other tools.

#!/bin/bash
paralleltool="parallel -j0"
cat <<'EOF' > mycommand
#!/bin/bash
# Generate 1 GB output
yes "`perl -e 'print "c"x30_000'`" | head -c 1G
EOF
chmod +x mycommand
# Run 20 jobs in parallel
# Adjust 20 to be > physical RAM and < free space on /tmp
seq 20 | time $paralleltool ./mycommand | wc -c

DISKFULL: Incomplete data if /tmp runs full

If caching is done on disk, the disk can run full during the run. Not all programs discover this. GNU Parallel discovers it, if it stays full for at least 2 seconds.

#!/bin/bash
paralleltool="parallel -j0"
# This should be a dir with less than 100 GB free space
smalldisk=/tmp/shm/parallel

TMPDIR="$smalldisk"
export TMPDIR

max_output() {
    # Force worst case scenario:
    # Make GNU Parallel only check once per second
    sleep 10
    # Generate 100 GB to fill $TMPDIR
    # Adjust if /tmp is bigger than 100 GB
    yes | head -c 100G >$TMPDIR/$$
    # Generate 10 MB output that will not be buffered
    # due to full disk
    perl -e 'print "X"x10_000_000' | head -c 10M
    echo This part is missing from incomplete output
    sleep 2
    rm $TMPDIR/$$
    echo Final output
}

export -f max_output
seq 10 | $paralleltool max_output | tr -s X

CLEANUP: Leaving tmp files at unexpected death

Some tools do not clean up tmp files if they are killed. If the tool buffers on disk, they may not clean up, if they are killed.

#!/bin/bash
paralleltool=parallel
ls /tmp >/tmp/before
seq 10 | $paralleltool sleep &
pid=$!
# Give the tool time to start up
sleep 1
# Kill it without giving it a chance to cleanup
kill -9 $!
# Should be empty: No files should be left behind
diff <(ls /tmp) /tmp/before

SPCCHAR: Dealing badly with special file names.

It is not uncommon for users to create files like:

My brother's 12" *** record  (costs $$$).jpg

Some tools break on this.

#!/bin/bash
paralleltool=parallel
touch "My brother's 12\" *** record  (costs $$$).jpg"
ls My*jpg | $paralleltool ls -l

COMPOSED: Composed commands do not work

Some tools require you to wrap composed commands into bash -c.

echo bar | $paralleltool echo foo; echo {}

ONEREP: Only one replacement string allowed

Some tools can only insert the argument once.

echo bar | $paralleltool echo {} foo {}

INPUTSIZE: Length of input should not be limited

Some tools limit the length of the input lines artificially with no good reason. GNU parallel does not:

perl -e 'print "foo."."x"x100_000_000' | parallel echo {.}

GNU parallel limits the command to run to 128 KB due to execve(1):

perl -e 'print "x"x131_000' | parallel echo {} | wc

NUMWORDS: Speed depends on number of words

Some tools become very slow if output lines have many words.

#!/bin/bash
paralleltool=parallel
cat <<-EOF > mycommand
#!/bin/bash
# 10 MB of lines with 1000 words
yes "`seq 1000`" | head -c 10M
EOF
chmod +x mycommand
# Run 30 jobs in parallel
seq 30 | time $paralleltool -j0 ./mycommand > /dev/null

4GB: Output with a line > 4GB should be OK

#!/bin/bash

paralleltool="parallel -j0"
wcc() {
    parallel --recend '' --block 100M --pipe 'LC_ALL=C wc' |
      datamash -W sum 1 sum 2 sum 3
}
cat <<-EOF > mycommand
#!/bin/bash

perl -e '\$a="a"x1000_000; for(1..5000) { print \$a }'
EOF
chmod +x mycommand

# Run 1 job
seq 1 | $paralleltool ./mycommand | wcc

DEPENDENCIES

GNU parallel uses Perl, and the Perl modules Getopt::Long, IPC::Open3, Symbol, IO::File, POSIX, and File::Temp. For remote usage it also uses rsync with ssh.
